1

“We’re back baby!” I said as I approached the crime scene tape strung across the exhibit.

The museum was supposed to have a plethora of beautiful art, but in this section, empty displays were piled with intricate swirls of dirt surrounded by law enforcement officers with an alphabet soup of abbreviations on their backs.

“Scharf!”

A black head of short hair popped up, and Special Agent Laura Scharf headed my way. She wore a blue nylon blazer stamped in yellow letters spelling out FBI over a white button-down shirt and black suit pants.

She smiled for half a second. “Glad you could make it.”

I clapped my hands together. “Where’s the fairy?”

This was the bargain we’d made: I help her with supernatural crimes in exchange for not arresting me. The sadistic scorekeeper in my head that kept track of these bargains had decided the scales were not balanced yet.

I looked around and let my hands drop. The complete lack of fairies, djinn, dragons, or goblins in the room meant this was outside of my jurisdiction.

Scharf shooed me back out of the exhibit and dangled pairs of gloves and booties in my face. I sighed and put them on. I’d lost the thou-must-wear-gloves argument at our first crime scene together a few months ago.

“Thanks for coming,” she repeated.

“There is absolutely nothing magical about stealing a bunch of dirt.” I pointed a gloved hand at the general melee.

“Lee is getting a nose for these things. When he said it was something special for me, I believe him.” She gestured to a familiar man in a ratty blue blazer looking at an empty pedestal in the glare of a police light. Though he had no letters on his back, I knew he was Metro PD because he was the first person to arrest me when I got to DC. It had been a rough year.

Though technically he arrested me on New Year’s Eve, so this year was going better? In three months, I’d destroyed Union Station, got arrested, and almost killed three times by a djinni, a goblin, and a dragon.

So…it could only go up from here?

I crossed my arms. “Lee thinks I’m your quirky roommate who should be in jail.”

She raised an eyebrow. “My expert consultant who occasionally, with no one the wiser, sleeps on my couch. And a Presidential pardon, so no more getting arrested.” She paused before reentering the archway into the empty exhibit. “Unless you’ve done something else he should arrest you for?”

I shook my head vigorously. I cast my mind over the last few weeks; it was even true. Since I’d defeated the challenger for my father’s throne, it had been a quiet month.

My father had been the king of goblins. From him, I’d inherited a box I still couldn’t open, a multitude of hobgoblin powers I was still figuring out, and his kingdom. Though that wasn’t as big a commitment as I originally assumed. Goblins don’t really go for group activities. Or authority figures.

Scharf waved at Detective Lee who was now speaking to somebody in a full-body white jumpsuit. I about-faced to retreat up the stairs.

Scharf took a slow deep breath and said in her FBI voice, “Orange.”

I pivoted back. “Seriously, what am I doing here? I can’t help gather evidence. Or magically divine… Whatever was in here.”

“Irish arts and crafts.”

I felt a whisper of disquiet when I heard Irish. I tugged at my very orange curls. My father was from Scotland, as were many magical creatures. I don’t know why we collected on tiny islands in the North Atlantic. I personally suspected it was the mist. You can get away with a lot of magic when it’s foggy.

Washington DC is not very foggy. Grey, cold, with this spitting sleet that TV weather personalities called “wintry mix,” but not foggy. So if the Irish were involved, there was an outside chance this was magical.

Except… “This is the American History Museum. Why is there a display of Irish arts and crafts?”

“Irish American arts and crafts,” Scharf said, “Made by immigrants or descendants. That part’s not clear.”

My shiver died. “Sticking an infinity knot on a jug does not make this Irish.”

There was nothing Irish about Scharf. She was German pale with black hair and stubborn cheekbones. Actually, every one of her bones was stubborn. Her stick-up-the-ass posture came from her time in the army though, not her heritage.

She took another step into the light, “We have work to do.”

“Sure, when there’s another murdering creature on the loose, I’m your goblin, but this is no business of the Nnn….whatever they’re calling it.”

“The National Taskforce on the Efficacy of Partnering with the Unexplained for Enforcing the Law,” Scharf said quickly.

“I can’t imagine why I didn’t remember that. Are you going to trade your blazer for one that says, um, NTEPUEL?”

Yes, the word was out, at least among the nation’s top-secret agencies that there was a good chance not everyone on Earth was human. Our last case was just a little too dramatic to hide the fact that Scharf was closing cases with supernatural help.

What did our nation’s most secret government servants do about this world-view-shattering fact?

If you guessed form a task force, you get a pony. In a moment of surviving-a-dragon-induced optimism, I agreed to join it. Well, that and Scharf sleeps with a gun. Not that she’d ever point it at me—again. I just wanted to mention that fact in relation to this decision.

It’s just that my expertise came from eleven semesters of folklore classes from the anthropology degree I’d abandoned and three months as the heir of the goblin king. If that sounds like insufficient preparation for protecting the country and defending the Constitution or whatever Scharf always went on about, then you’re right.

“Lee can’t even see me!”

She said through gritted teeth, “You can’t stay invisible forever.”

She wasn’t speaking metaphorically. I always could disappear and know the escape route out of every situation. I was 100% okay with goblins being the cowards of the supernatural community.

As I thought of escaping, an orange line only I could see led out of the exhibit and up the stairs to the eight glass doors at the front of the museum, presenting me with the fastest escape route.

Yes, thank you, I thought to my magic with a roll of my eyes. The line disappeared.

“Scharf!” Detective Lee said as he hitched up his pants and came over. “You came alone? This almost makes me miss Orange.” He glanced around quickly. “You didn’t bring her, did you?”

From my angle, I could see her right-hand flex, one of her only tells. Sometimes she couldn’t control the enthusiasm of her weapon hand.

“I can solve crimes without consultants.”

I played it back in my head. It was technically not a lie. I didn’t know whether to feel proud or upset that she was getting sneakier. Scharf was the most honest person I knew; she never lied until she met me.

Lee rocked forward on his toes. “I don’t know if any of us can solve this one without somebody’s help.”

I sighed and retreated out of his direct view and let go of the focus that let me go unnoticed. That was technically what happened. I didn’t disappear from sight; it’s just that no one noticed me.

No one except Scharf. I hadn’t successfully disappeared in front of her in weeks. Scharf said that she never took her eyes off a threat. She found that uproariously funny every time she said it. I laughed too because, you know, she’s armed.

I stepped closer and waved at Lee, who did not have Scharf’s poker face. He squeaked and jumped. I didn’t blame him. I disappeared out of his locked police car and handcuffs the first time I figured out this unnoticing thing. He didn’t take it well.

“Orange!” he said with false heartiness. “Didn’t see you there.”

I bit my lip. “How strange. I’ve been standing here the whole time.”

I am not as good a person as Scharf.

“Why are we here?” Scharf asked.

Lee scratched at his chin and pointed. “Come look at this.”

We walked over to the central exhibit, a low wide pedestal in white, now covered in dirt. I read the plaque next to it. It had a picture of the artifact that had been on the dais. A beautiful, mahogany table and chair set with elaborately carved backs in a definite Celtic style. “Appalachian table and four chairs. Circa 1900s.”

“Look,” Lee insisted.

With Scharf, I leaned over the dais to stare at the dirt and saw a five-pointed star traced with elaborate knots and swirls in the dirt.

I laughed; I couldn’t help it.

Scharf, Lee, and a couple of techs looked at me.

“Sorry.” I pointed to the picture. “The backs of the chair had the same design: a star amongst leaves. And knots.”

“So this is not a satanic ritual?” Lee asked. “A couple years ago, a kid carved pentagrams into his friend in a satanic ritual. I mean, it wasn't a satanic ritual. He was just awful, but he said that’s what he was trying to do. I was still on the beat.”

I shook my head. This is one subject we had covered in my folklore classes, probably to forestall this exact conversation when students found pentagrams everywhere. “I’m just guessing here, but pentagrams are found in almost every culture. Most of the time, they have pretty positive connotations.”

Scharf crossed her arms. “Like what?”

“In Celtic mythology? No one knows for sure because the druids didn’t write anything down. They think it’s a symbol of the feminine. All stages of womanhood: maiden, mother, crone, light, and dark. That’s just the Celts. You would get a totally different answer in India, China, the Middle East, Africa.”

“That’s a lot of different options,” Scharf said at last, and she looked around at other displays.

“They all have designs on them,” Lee said. “But this is the only one I recognized. We’re getting pictures of it all. It might mean nothing if it was the designs on the artwork. I’ll have to get the exhibit catalog. I didn’t think to match the dirt with the art.”

He sounded annoyed that he hadn’t. He’d just seen what he thought was the devil and panicked and called us. I hoped it wasn’t the devil. I hoped there wasn’t a devil, in general. I was dating a demon, but we didn’t really talk about who he worked for. Mostly Jack just cooked at his restaurant and occasionally helped me kill ghuls.

“No satanic worship?” Lee repeated. “I’m not going to find a dead body staked to that table tomorrow?”

Scharf looked at me. I started to nod, but an uncharacteristic caution gripped me. “I have no idea.” I tried to stop talking but couldn’t help myself. “But I don’t think so. I don’t know if this is…”

“If this is your area of expertise?” Scharf prompted.

“Sorry. Like I said, your thief could just be stealing all of this and messing with us by replacing it with dirt. It’s kind of poetic.”

“Poetic?”

“You know from dust to dust and all that?”

“Sir!”

We turned to see a tall guy in a brown police uniform that looked nothing like the dark blue Metro PD wore.

He waved from the stairs. “Sir, there’s something you should see.”

“This is Adelman from Park Police,” Lee said.

I frowned. “What park?”

The young man pivoted again, flashing me a gap-toothed smile that made him look younger than me. “The National Mall. America’s front lawn.”

“Cool!” I said when he clearly expected a response. He grinned and pivoted to bound up the stairs.

“This museum has its own police?”

Scharf smiled. “All of the Smithsonians.”

We followed him out of the exhibit around an old ship propped up on similar white pedestals. Apparently, the thieves didn’t want a decaying boat to go with their crafts. We walked back up wide, white stairs to the main foyer of the shuttered museum as Adelman regaled me with the reason he joined the park police and not Metro PD.

“I mean, with the protests, I’m not sorry, you know?”

I nodded vigorously. I’d been way too close to the recent dragon-induced riots that had gripped the city. Not that we had advertised the dragon part.

When we reached the foyer, I goggled at the three banners dangling from the ceiling: one of the American flag, one with a neon pink leotard whose antecedents weren’t clear, and one with an infinity knot in spun glass stamped with the words “Special Exhibit” and “St. Patrick’s Day.”

“This way,” Adelman gestured us over to an unmarked door painted white to match the walls near the metal detectors in the huge glass entryway.

Inside, a guy in jeans and a sweatshirt stood at a bank of monitors behind an old-school beige computer with a tricked-out black keyboard, plus a few TV remotes. He looked at us through thick glasses and blinked owlishly. “Uh, just give me a second.”

Adelman shifted from foot to foot with excitement. His body was concave in his mud-brown uniform. This close, I could see the fuzz of his beard as well. He might have actually been younger than my twenty-four years.

“What are we going to see?” Lee asked.

“It’s weird,” Adelman said with relish.

“It is?” Scharf asked in alarm.

“It’s a tape,” Adelman said and flung out a hand. “This is Mr. Turner, one of our digital security experts.”

“And this is my day off,” he offered the room without looking at us. “Don’t know what the hell happened to O’Connor.”

Lee shifted. “O’Connor?”

“O’Connor called out,” Adelman said.

Lee fished out his cell phone. “We have another missing security guard. No, he called out. He wasn’t on the schedule we used.”

Turner straightened. “You think O’Connor pulled off the biggest art heist in the history of this museum? O’Connor. He could barely find the break room.”

“We need to find him, sir,” Lee said in his matter-of-fact voice which meant this was an emergency. To his phone, he said, “Get the suit on the phone from the main museum office and send squad cars to his house ASAP.”

The guy blinked at us and then went back to the screens, typing away at the computer as cameras sped up and changed between various exhibits around the museum on the monitors. I saw a military helicopter on a dais, bizarre heavy curtains in the shape of a dress, and others that went by too quickly to discern.

“Another security guard?” Scharf asked Lee.

“Yeah, one no-showed, and, apparently, one called out.”

Turner carefully hit one of the buttons on a remote with the tip of his index finger like it was going to shock him. Adelman bounced on the balls of his feet and said, “Here’s what you need to see.”

The screens stopped cycling and settled on different views of an intact Irish American arts and crafts exhibit.

I whistled, impressed despite myself. I’d joked about infinity knots on a jug but most of the stuff onscreen was exquisite. A panel of tiles, green with a gold patina in an intricate pattern dominated the wall opposite the entrance while a multitude of smaller displays held glasswork and ceramic goblets without an obvious Celtic theme. I could see why it was called crafts, not just art. These were practical pieces, meant to be used, not just stuck in a museum.

“What time is this?” Lee asked.

Turner pointed to the running clock in the bottom corner: 3:03 AM.

We watched the silent exhibit for a few more minutes when a figure in black tiptoed into the display carrying a bag, followed by a lot more men in dark clothes. I gasped and the assembly looked sharply at me.

“What did you see?” Lee asked.

I frantically scanned the faces of the others. Turner was fidgeting with his phone, but the rest stared at me without expression. Nobody said, “Look at all the people running through the display. I guess we found our thieves.”

“Nothing, just, uh, rolled my ankle.” I stood on one leg for a second.

Scharf frowned but the rest went back to the screen.

The room onscreen was crawling with people: short, fair, dark-haired men wearing dark clothes and hats. And they were all grinning. They were carrying heavy bags into the exhibit, which confused me until I remembered the dirt. It had to get in there somehow.

I tugged on Scharf’s sleeve.

“Nothing is happening,” she said to me with a raised eyebrow.

“No shit,” Lee replied.

The men stopped their dance, and I tried to get a quick count; it had to be almost two dozen guys. They were far shorter than I, probably around four feet tall, which meant they were insanely strong or had earth magic like me. They didn’t look like goblins. Aside from the fact that they were invisible, they looked human. They stood at attention, each near a different display. A few stuck their hands into their bags.

A hand appeared in view. Its owner stood just under the camera, and that’s all I could see of him. He snapped his fingers.

The men threw dirt into the air and snatched at the artifacts, one carrying the entire wall of tile by himself. The dirt fell in intricate swirls with a wave of their fingers. They definitely could manipulate earth like a goblin.

The room reacted in gasps and shouts.

“Play it back!” Lee demanded. “Now!”

2

Turner hit the rewind button.

I ground my teeth. We watched the little men toss the dirt and snatch the artifacts again. Again Turner stopped it before I could watch the whole thing play out.

I resisted telling them to keep going. I wanted to see the man who belonged to the hand. The finger snapper obviously had control of the room.

We watched a third time and finally, he let it run. The men trooped out with the artifacts, came back, and did it again to the next display and the next until the entire exhibit was empty and covered with patterned dirt. They didn’t trace anything in the dirt manually; it just landed in crazy patterns. The head guy stayed out of camera range. I never saw him.

I tugged Scharf’s arm again. She ignored me.

“Turner, can you get me the tape on this?” Lee asked. “We’ll need to see how they doctored that.”

“I guess so.”

Adelman shook his head. “Our servers are meant to be tamper-proof, sir.”

“So is your alarm system, your rotating guards, your locks, and every other inch of this museum, but somebody got past all of it,” Lee said.

“You want the Bureau to take the footage?” Scharf asked.

Lee crossed his arms. “You implying the Metropolitan Police Department can’t handle a simple security footage splice?”

Scharf rolled her eyes. “You know I don’t care about pissing contests. I was asking if you needed our help.”

He ratcheted his shoulders down. “I know, I know. Neither do I. But the boys in cybercrimes do love pissing matches, and if they heard I sent it off to the Feds, they’d be—put out. Let ‘em at it. Besides, if there’s one thing they excel at in this town, it’s security footage.”

“Fair enough.”

I cleared my throat. “I’d like to take another look at that pentagram. Scharf?”

Lee put the phone to his ear. “We’ll be sending you some footage. I need an answer today. Yes, this is absolutely a priority. Well, the FBI is standing here wondering when they can take it…”

He smiled as he hung up. “Thanks again for offering.”

Scharf grinned. “We are happy to liaise in every way we can with local law enforcement agencies.”

She wasn’t being sarcastic. One thing I’d been surprised by, working on several investigations, was how often and easily agencies worked together. At least in DC. Here, the world of trench coats and turf wars was the exception rather than the rule. Probably because the amount of overlapping responsibility for every inch of this place was four or five agencies deep.

“Can I take some of the dirt though?” Scharf asked. “I have a great dirt expert.”

He snorted. “Please, please take some of the dirt.”

“Scharf, a consult, please,” I said.

Lee looked mildly interested.

“I can perhaps find other ancient mythological stories to match up with the rest of the exhibit!” I told him with bright enthusiasm.

He turned back to the cameras. “Have fun with that!”

Scharf nodded, and we left the little security office. I looked around and tugged her into a hallway under the American flag banner. It was dark and I wished I had super senses to check if we were alone, but those did not seem to be a talent of goblins.

Well, unless I made a bargain to get that specific information. Most goblins have access to their powers through bargains with humans. They always had to help the human in exchange. Since I was half-human, I could bargain with myself for access to magic any time, but goblin magic is wily, and I was still learning how to balance both sides to make sure the bargain didn’t come back and bite me. With my own teeth.

Scharf cleared her throat and my eyes snapped back to hers. “It wasn’t doctored footage.”

“What did you see?”

“What did you see?” I retorted.

A deep booming voice said from the foyer, “You said they went that way?”

Scharf stood up straighter, a thing I would have said was impossible moments before. I slunk deeper into the shadows and thought hard, Don’t see me.

She strode out of the hallway and said, “Director!”

I groaned and followed, still unnoticeable. I hoped.

Deputy Assistant Director Martin Giudice stood with his feet planted and hands on his hips in the center of the foyer. He worked in the Hoover Building at the National FBI Headquarters in the national counterterrorism division. He was the one who’d recruited Scharf and me by extension onto his fancy unexplainable task force.

He looked like the kind of person who starts task forces. Roman nose, spray tan, buzzcut, and the beginnings of a beer gut, wearing an off-the-rack suit and an expensive watch. He looked around her for a second and my heart stopped, but his gaze passed by me.

“I was told it was a special exhibit, not the flag.”

Scharf blinked. “I’m sorry, sir?”

He pointed. The hallway we’d retreated to led into the American flag exhibit.

Scharf said, “Trying to get… Reception, sir.” She took the phone out of the clip on her belt, stared at it, and put it back.

“Your source did not come through?” he asked, his voice mild.

“My source? Oh, my source.” Scharf’s eyes didn’t so much as flicker toward me. “I didn’t call them. Wanted to evaluate the scene first.”

He rocked back on his heels. “And evaluate me.”

She blinked.

“I understand. One of the other ones we work with took five years to, er, come out.”

My eyes grew huge in my head. I mouthed, “The other ones?”

Giudice rocked back on his heels with a false jocularity I hated. “One of the old ones,” he said, “took far longer than that. One of us says welcome, and I don’t expect them to play fetch.”

I threw my hands wide. “See? He gets me!”

She didn’t say my name in her FBI voice, but I heard it just the same.

He cracked his knuckles. “So, walk me through this.”

Scharf said, “I’m sorry?”

He grinned. “I wondered if you could say anything except, ‘Yes, sir.’ I’m counterterrorism. Our whole mission is to prevent crime scenes. You’re violent crimes, correct? Why am I here?”

“Frankly, sir, I didn’t expect you to come at all. I was just giving you a heads up that I had received word from a local about a potential—situation.” She petered out.

“Your message reached me when I reached the office. Since you are literally next door, I thought I’d skip seven o’clock and see what’s what.”

“He can skip entire hours?” I asked.

Scharf flicked at her ear but did not answer me. “So you’re using a crime scene to get out of a meeting.”

“Absolutely. Catch me up.”

Scharf narrowed her eyes. She’d recently gotten into a fair bit of trouble for being honest with her superiors. And I use that word in the technical sense since they were inferior in every other way except rank.

I made fun of the task force, but frankly, I’d put her career on life support during our last few cases, and this deputy associate assistant or whatever was throwing her a lifesaver, or at least a career saver. She’d spent a lot of the last two weeks lecturing me on the proper way to address an assistant associate. Honestly, that discussion more than anything had prompted my disappearing act. I didn’t want to mess up anything more for her.

She cleared her throat. “I should point out that I do violent crime, not usually robberies.”

Giudice didn’t even look at her as he said, “Noted.”

“He didn’t actually note that,” I said.

Lee exited the security office and said, “It’s just crazy. Cybercrimes will be here in twenty.”

Scharf smiled and stepped away from Giudice. “Detective Lee, I’d like you to meet Martin Giudice, counterterrorism. Detective Daniel Lee, Robbery Homicide, Metro PD.”

Lee whistled low. “Sir, this is not terrorism. It’s strange as hell but nobody’s jumped to terror. Has there been threats against our museums?”

“Not at all Detective, I’m working on a task force that focuses on the strange as hell things we see on the job.”

“Ooh, do I have stories for you!” Lee said with enthusiasm that made the blood drain from my face. I frantically waved at Scharf.

She forced a smile. “Let’s save those for the happy hour when this is solved?”

Giudice ignored her. “We occasionally recruit members from various disciplines. I haven’t had anyone from Metro PD, but I could see the value in bringing in local police. Especially from the nation’s capital.”

Lee looked as horrified as I did, though probably for different reasons. I mentally added the penchant for referring to DC as the nation’s capital to Giudice’s flaws. I was surprised he hadn’t run for office yet with those kinds of rhetorical quirks.

Lee said, “I don’t think the boss is going to go for that.”

Giudice shrugged. The shrug meant: Yes, he will.

“Detective?” Scharf said quietly.

Lee cleared his throat. “Well, we have some doctored footage to puzzle out.”

“Potentially,” Scharf added. “Potentially doctored.”

Giudice raised an eyebrow.

Scharf swallowed. “And we have some dirt.”

“Some dirt?” His tone said: I should not have skipped seven o’clock for this.

I winced and said, “Tell him about the pentagram.”

“They traced patterns into the dirt, the main one being a pentagram on the primary display.”

He perked right up. “Was this some kind of weird ritual? Maybe Satanists?” He seemed pretty happy about that too.

Scharf shook her head. “The pentagram or pentac…”

“Pentacle!’

“Pentacle is a symbol found in many, many cultures.”

Lee scratched his head. “It’s an Irish art exhibit, sir. Arts and crafts. The star was on the furniture they stole. They copied that design, and many others in the dirt they left behind.”

“Arts and crafts,” he echoed. “So this is just a robbery scene?”

“We don’t have enough information to say at this time,” Scharf said, sounding pained. “I really only called you as an FYI.”

“Okay.” Giudice rocked back on his heels. “I’m sure the Art Crimes team is deploying as we speak.” He huffed. “I believe this is the shortest distance they’ve ever had to travel.”

“Yes, sir.”

He clapped Scharf on the back. “Thanks for checking this out. No rock left unturned. Our meeting is scheduled for Thursday, correct?”

“Yes, sir. Seven o’clock.”

I really hoped he didn’t skip that seven o’clock.

He strode out the glass doors, barely waiting for a security guard in a brown uniform identical to Adelman’s to get them open.

Lee blinked. “What was that all about?”

“Task forces and the Hoover Building,” Scharf said, not without some bitterness. “Translation: politics and bullshit.”

Lee looked around, “Where’s your friend?”

Scharf just shook her head. “She disappeared on me.”

I sighed. Here was the Scharf I knew, doling out the literal truth, no matter how much it hurt.

A guy in a white clean suit jogged up the stairs. “You wanted a sample?”

Lee pointed to Scharf, and he handed over a little baggy of dirt in an elaborate ritual of clipboards and signoffs.

“When will you start clearing out?” Lee asked him.

“The feds want to cut the displays out and take them intact to their lab.” He glanced at Scharf. “Your lab.”

“The feds are here?” Scharf asked.

The guy scratched his head through the cowl, “Y’all don’t talk to each other?”

Scharf tucked the sample into her pocket. “Thanks for this.”

She headed for the door.

“You don’t want to do the wrap-up with us?” Lee asked.

She screwed a smile to her face. “Art crimes is here. They can take it. Make sure they see the tape.”

I waited until Lee had disappeared back down the stairs, even though he wouldn’t have heard when I said, “Sorry.”

Scharf glanced at me and offered me that same fake smile. “You didn’t call Art Crimes. What did you see—” She trailed off when a gaggle of people in suits strode past talking into walkie-talkies about press statements and remembering to put a closed sign on the door.

Scharf cocked a head and made for the glass doors at the entrance and waited until the security guard let us out into the brightening day on the National Mall.

She said nothing as we crossed the wide cement walkway past a bizarre statue of a giant silver ribbon. I thought we were heading to her department-issued purple Buick, but we passed that sitting in a parking spot the size of a tour bus on the frontage road between the museums and the main lawn.

She crossed the road and started down the wide graveled path cut into the lawn until we stood in the center of the Mall in the shadow of the Washington Monument to our right and a hazy view of the Capitol at the other end of the Mall.

“So you’re not on the case?” I asked incredulously.

She sighed. “Normally, a violent crimes agent wouldn’t have come anywhere near an art theft.”

“But the task force…”

“Isn’t going to win that pissing contest, not if Giudice thinks it’s dirt, arts, and crafts.”

I crossed my arms. “Even if magical invisible men stole all the art?”

She turned to me. “Is that what you saw on that tape?”

“What did you see?”

“One moment the artifacts were there. The next they disappeared, and dirt was falling through the air. It looked like someone spliced the tape.”

“I saw an army of little people grab everything in that exhibit and walk out with it. And leave the dirt behind.”

“What?” Scharf’s hand dropped to her side in simple shock.

“And somebody snapped,” I added.

“They attacked?”

I frowned, then snapped my fingers. “Snapped snapped. There seemed to be a ringleader controlling them, but he stayed out of shot.”

“Did you recognize any of them?”

I rolled my eyes. “I don’t know all the magical creatures on Earth. Some very short, pale men with dark hair and hats and clothes came in and grabbed the artifacts, and then threw the dirt down. Then they left when somebody who didn’t show up in camera range snapped their fingers. His fingers. Or a woman with really thick fingers.”

“And no ring?” A ring had screwed us in one of our last cases.

I shook my head. “I don’t think he needed one.”

She started to walk at a pace that would have left me quickly behind, so I just waited until she came back. She was never not moving. At home, she spent her free time on a treadmill.

Scharf finally turned back and said, “That’s all you saw?”

I closed my eyes, trying to think back. “They were giggling. They were having a marvelous time.” I opened my eyes. “I think you did see the men. I think you just didn’t notice them. It was a recording. For me to see them, they had to be on that tape.”

She started to pace, stopped, and turned back to me. “Orange.”

My heart picked up speed. “They weren’t goblins!” I hope.

She looked at the sky, now fading from pinks and grays to the anemic blue of a DC sunny day. “You’re sure.”

“They were way too pretty,” I scuffed one toe in the dirt.

“Orange, you are pretty.”

“Thanks for the vote of confidence. You know it’s only an illusion.” It was literally an illusion. To me, changing my appearance felt the same as going unnoticed. I could just adjust what people saw when they looked at me.

I defrizzed my hair and toned down the shade of my skin from fake tan orange to a real tan and rounded my fingernails. Probably the most obvious sign of my heritage was the floppy, gnawed points at the end of my ears. I didn’t have to glamour those because I just kept them always hidden in my hair. I’d spent the first two decades of life thinking they were a birth defect.

“Besides, what would a goblin do with a warehouse full of arts and crafts?” I asked and then frowned. “Why would anyone want a warehouse full of arts and crafts?”

“We’ll have to figure that out.” She scrubbed her hands through her hair and tugged. “Art crimes will have to figure that out.”

She did another loop. “So what did they look like if they weren’t goblins?”

“They wore black, including hats.”

“But you could see their faces?”

I nodded. “Why hide when you know you won’t show up?”

“Good point. What else?”

“White skin. Super short.”

She glanced at me. “White? Like ghost white?”

“Metaphorically white, literally beige. They looked Caucasian and human, except invisible. And short. Scout’s honor.” I winced. “I should mention I got kicked out of scouts for switching out the matches for sparklers during my first campout.”

She laughed, startling a jogger in excessive spandex. Another car pulled up in front of the museum and disgorged five armed, excessively fit dudes who jogged toward the doors.

“The cavalry has arrived?” I asked.

“We should get out of here,” Scharf said and started back toward her car.

She got in, and I dropped into the passenger seat and quickly buckled up, patting the panic bar as I did.

Scharf’s compulsion to be moving extended to her driving; that and her government license plate meant she pretty much went as fast as possible with nothing and nobody to rein her in.

But she didn’t start the car, just sat toying with her keys.

“What?” I asked after twenty seconds of this.

“I can’t walk away.”

I grinned. “I knew it! We need to get another look at that tape, and then…”

“But I have to walk away.”

I gaped at her. “Who are you and what have you done with Laura Scharf?”

“It’s not just me anymore. What do I tell Giudice? Or art crimes? Or my actual boss?”

“What they want to hear?”

“I can’t go back and tell him I ignored his orders, consulted with my source, and it was an army of invisible little people that only she can see.”

“I wonder…” I trailed off.

“Yeah?”

“If now you could see them. If I pointed them out, would you notice them? When I’m invisible, if somebody touches me or suddenly notices me somehow, they can see me.”

“But do you show up on camera?”

I frowned. “I’ve never tested it before.”

She stared blindly through the windshield for a second before she hit the steering wheel. “We’ll have to risk it. You’ll just have to point them out at the meeting.”

“I can just…” Her words registered. “At the meeting?”

A tiny smile touched her lips. “Seven o’clock? Orange, you promised.”

I resisted telling her that a promise is different from a bargain. I could back out of a promise. “Okay, yes. Go to the meeting. Smile and nod at the feds. Not tell them about invisible thieves! And then have to explain why I can see invisible people… I agreed to come. Not to come out!”

She sighed. “I know. And I am not going to force you. It’s a terrible thing to do, whether you’re gay,” she pointed to herself, “or you know…” She waved at me.

“A half-magical creature with destructive powers? You could also go back to plan A and not get involved? You’re always complaining that these things get dumped on you, but now the Art Thief FBI people are involved, you could just. . .” I stared at the Washington Monument visible through the windshield. Halfway up the stone changed. “It looks stupid with two different color stones.”

“I don’t think they did it on purpose.” She added half-heartedly, “I can’t let this go.”

I fiddled with a stray thread from my jeans. “I figured.”

Finally, she started the car and swung out of the spot without really checking behind her. We hung two rights in quick succession without regard to lights and bulleted down Constitution Avenue.

We turned left, heading north toward her office downtown. She worked in a DC field office in the violent crimes division, not the national Hoover Building, fortunately. It was a small office suite tucked in a random building a few streets away. The N had fallen off the door, so everyone called it the Violet Crimes office, the unofficial name for all the weird crimes she was getting involved with. It sounded way better than NTEPUEL.

As we waited at a light, Scharf said, “Look, let’s worry about the meeting when it happens. No matter what we do, this may not ever be our case, but can you at least contact your own sources and see what humanoid, short, metaphorically white, creatures can disappear and like arts and crafts and dirt?”

“That will narrow down the list. What are you going to do?”

“After I figure out whether I can poke my nose into this? We do know an ace dirt analyst.”

Maria Hernandez from the FBI lab at Quantico had helped us with the mysterious sand that covered our first crime scene.

As she took off again, and I squeezed the panic bar, she said, “Look, the first time I went to the Hoover Building, I sweated through my shirt.”

My jaw dropped. Scharf had fought in a war and caught bad guys for a living. Nothing phased her.

“It’s just another collection of agents, just people. I promise nothing bad will happen to you.”

I threw up my hands. “Now you’ve done it.”

3

I got off the metro at Columbia Heights and weaved through streets of big box stores before I made it to the neighborhood behind them of old row houses with chain-link square concrete front yards and painted wrought iron bars on every window. The shop fronts changed from hip vegan bakeries and coffee shops to cochinas and cafes filled to the brim with empanadas and arepas.

My main “source” in the magical community consisted of one ancient woman from Colombia, who may or may not have been some kind of rain goddess. She’d never said, though the weather had a suspicious way of doing exactly what she wanted around her townhouse.

No magic was necessary today. It was a beautiful morning, crisp but fragrant with flowers that seemed to explode from every other tree planted along most sidewalks. Juya sat in a folding chair behind two plastic tables under a colorful patio umbrella. The tables were groaning under the baked goods piled in front of her.

“Duende!” Juya said with a wave. It meant goblin, the first word she’d said to me when she’d told me what I am. She looked to be a thousand years old with deep wrinkles lining her brown skin and hair almost white in the low bun at the base of her neck. She wore a long jean skirt and a puffy orange jacket last sold in the eighties. A large flat hat sat on her coiled silver hair. She didn’t know when she came to exist, but I knew it was long before Amerigo made a map of the continent that would be named after him.

“Como estas?” Juya asked as I approached her table and pointed to an empanada.

I glanced around the little courtyard scattered with wrought iron tables and chairs for customers. A couple sat wrapped up in each other, literally and metaphorically. A young guy inhaled a plate of cake tucked behind a biology laboratory manual called “The Circle of Life.” None of them were paying attention.

I glanced behind Juya into the beginnings of the shop. They were converting the downstairs of two townhouses into a cafe. I could hear salsa playing from the boarded-up main window where the glass hadn’t come in yet, but I couldn’t see any of the crew.

I turned back to Juya. “I’m good. How are you doing?”

It wasn’t an idle question. She’d looked near death’s door the last time I saw her. But she looked as if nothing had happened today.

She harrumphed and handed me an empanada. When I’d first hit DC, this California girl had missed her tacos. Most of the Latin population of DC hailed from South America, not Central, so instead of avocados and green chiles, you got pork belly and plantains. I hadn’t turned my nose up on good food for long. I looked longingly at the array of cakes on the table, but she shook her head. “You need your strength.”

“I’m a goblin! We need our sugar.”

Someone coughed from behind me.

I stepped to the side so the guy in a suit that was a little too tight could get his breakfast—a piece of tres leches in a small Styrofoam bowl, which he ate in huge gulps as he jogged back out the gate and turned left toward the metro. Then she served a student, judging by the massive backpack on her back. She got a pile of pastries I didn’t know the name for and settled across from Biology dude with a murmur.

“How come they don’t need their strength?”

Juya raised an eyebrow at me.

I shuffled my feet. “When will the big debut be? Do you have a name?”

Juya had been a fixture in this community for as long as anyone could remember, selling her confections for cash. But when she’d missed a few weeks, the community stopped taking her for granted. A few local business owners were helping her set up a full-blown shop. Cash flow wasn’t a problem; when you’ve existed longer than the dollar or the peso, compound interest got interesting. She just never believed anyone would want more from her.

“This fuss is silly,” she said as if she’d read my thoughts (something I hadn’t ruled in or out). “Osman wants to ship café from Colombia directly. Something about fair trading. How do you stay in business if you don’t trade fair?”

I polished off the pastry and shook my head. “You would think that, wouldn’t you?” I brought up ammunition I know would motivate her. “It will make people happy.”

She smiled, doubling the wrinkles across her cheeks. “You are a tricky duende. What flavors will you sell in my shop?”

I hunched and looked down at the end of the table I’d been avoiding: my tiny contributions to her enterprise. Baggies filled with wet and dry ingredients paired together with instructions printed on address labels. They were mug cakes. The address labels were because of Astrid, my business partner. That was what she called herself.

She was not really my business partner, because that would require having a business. She had a business plan, and the actual business was me making up treats and sticking them on the end of Juya’s table for her to sell and Astrid sometimes hauling experiments to the freshman dorms at her university. Freshmen with only access to microwaves will eat anything.

“You’ll have to ask Astrid, I’m just the…” She’d given me a title in this business plan, but I’d already forgotten it. Or blocked it from my mind. Some kind of officer.

“But you will sell them!”

My shoulders reached my ears. “Yes, okay, fine.”

“Duende!” Osmund, Juya’s helper pointed to me as he came out the front door. I forced a smile on my face. He had always been jumpy about me, for no other reason than the goblin part. He was a Colombian man in his thirties who’d done most of the carpentry for this new shop. We had reached a marginal truce after I’d helped Juya heal, so the alarm in his voice was annoying.

“Duende!” Juya echoed and I realized he hadn’t been talking about me.

I pivoted slowly.

And looked down. A wizened goblin three feet tall with skin the color of walnuts and hair the color of clouds stood in a dirty jumper staring straight at me. The edge of his ears was ragged like mine, though twice as long.

“Peallaidh!” he croaked. He pronounced it peal-lid, which was not the worst butchering of the Gaelic I’d ever heard. Peallaidh, pronounced, roughly, pow-lich, was my father’s last name. At least, I’d thought it was my father’s last name. It was also his title.

Or stage name? There was no real equivalent in the normal world. The closest I could think of was professional wrestling or drag, the name famous performers take on becomes all they are known by. If he had another name, it was long forgotten with the centuries. Magical creatures are long-lived.

I crouched down to get to his eye level and said, “Absolutely not. I conquered the most among you. I’m not fighting you to the death! I did that already!”

He turned a much lighter shade of brown and his ear drooped. “Fight?”

I cocked my head. “You don’t want to fight me?”

He shook his head, his ears whipping around his face. For weeks, I couldn’t turn around without a goblin challenging me to make sure I was a worthy successor to my father. This was the first interaction with a goblin who didn’t want to conquer me in weeks.

“Well, then what do you want?”

“I seek the aid of the king…” I frowned. “Quee…” I raised an eyebrow. He tried again. “Princess?”

“Oh hell no,” I said, backing away.

Juya let out a cackling laugh. “Princess!”

I turned back. “No!”

A tear trickled down the wrinkles in his cheeks. I looked between the weeping goblin and Juya, marched over and grabbed a cocada off the table, and shoved it at him. He started to wail.

I squinted. “You are not turning down sugar. You are a goblin!”

His squeaky voice rang out. “I am not worthy.”

A pair of tourists approached the gate, and Juya waved them away with a muttered, “Ahora, no!”

I threw up my hands. “Now my problems are losing you business?”

“I said not now, not no,” Juya said with another chuckle.

I turned back to the little goblin. He now looked a little mottled. I was happy I didn’t change colors that much. “What exactly are you doing here? And don’t lead with the P-word.”

He blinked twice. “Peallaidh?”

“No, that P-word is okay. What do you want?”

“I seek the aid of my p—peallaidh.”

“He’s dead.”

“But, but, but… You are his p— person.”

My fellow goblins had challenged me and rejected me and laughed at me and I’d beaten them all. (Technically, I’d found someone stronger than them all and beaten him. Fortunately, finally, they’d been cowed by association.) But since then they’d melted away. I’d claimed my father’s kingdom, but just what that meant, they hadn’t seen fit to tell me. I had no idea what being the P-word entailed, the daughter of the last goblin king.

I guess I was about to find out.

“You want my help?” I resisted the surge of warmth. None of them had ever wanted my help.

“A bargain!” He regained a little dignity at the horrifying statement that he would get something for nothing. Yes, this was a goblin. Or was it? Small, wrinkly, wearing rags.

Wearing rags… “You’re a brownie!”

“Yes?”

I’d as yet only encountered the tiny creatures that keep houses and flee at the sight of education, baptism, or real clothes in my Celtic folklore classes, though there were similar house sprites of various descriptions in traditions around the world. I hadn’t known until this moment that they actually existed. Or that we were related.

I examined his wizened, exaggerated features. “Where did you come from? How did you get here?”

“I… I…” He started to tear up again.

That he was wandering around DC and not churning butter at whatever home he claimed meant something was seriously wrong. If I could trust the Book of Highland Lore.

A guy with an “I love DC” shirt, a fanny pack, sandals, and a camera around his neck stopped at the gate. He was muttering something about hearing about this place not in any guidebook.

I tugged the brownie to the right of Juya’s setup to a small, round iron table nobody wanted to sit at because it was off-kilter.

I sat so we were closer in height and glanced at Juya, whose wrinkles had rearranged themselves she was grinning so hard. She toasted me with a squeeze bottle of arequipe and turned to serve the guy.

The brownie was only inches taller than the table. He slowly chewed on the coconut treat, but when he caught me looking, he swallowed it whole. It traveled down his esophagus in a lump. I swallowed in sympathy.

“How can I help?” I asked him. The feeling felt so foreign and good to see one of my own kind and not brace for a fight.

“You help?” The look of unexpected hope on his face hurt.

“If I can?” I said and immediately regretted it. Just what was he asking?

He glanced away, unable to meet my eyes. I wasn’t sure if he was just intimidated by my family tree or if he was planning on scamming me. “He would have vanquished the foe.”

“He, my father?” I tapped a foot. “What foe?”

He hunched his shoulders and said in a voice pitched higher and higher, “He would have tried?”

I loved my father, but there’s a reason goblins aren’t usually the heroes in a story. We don’t vanquish foes. We don’t try. “Tried what? Be specific, please?”

“Made him stop!” This last ended on a howl. I flinched and looked around the courtyard. The obnoxious tourist looked like he was having an orgasm at the taste of the caramel. None of them were exclaiming that there was a miniature man that didn’t look quite human screaming at the top of his lungs. Though the biology student looked at me sideways.

I guessed the brownie and I had a few more things in common; nobody could see him. Self-consciously, I focused to make everybody forget I was there too. Now I knew how Scharf felt when someone caught her talking to the air.

“He has to stop.” The little brownie danced from foot to foot, making the tattered tunic sway around his spindly legs as he sniffled.

I patted him awkwardly on the shoulder. I wanted to help; I just hoped it would come with fewer tears. “Tell me the whole story.”

He brightened; we liked stories. “A stranger barged into home. No hello. And then he offered sewing lessons!”

I blinked. “And?”

“Sewing lessons,” he repeated, voice aghast.

I squinted, spooling back textbooks in my mind. Brownies spurned education and clothing and religion. “Ohhh.”

In one fell swoop, this stranger had offered education and potential clothing, a high insult. Which made me wonder if this stranger knew exactly what kind of creature he was dealing with.

But why would anyone want to get rid of a brownie? Yes, if a goblin was living with you, you may walk into a few more practical jokes, but we generally make a house better. Scharf was mostly happy she got unending baked goods when I stayed with her and was mostly resigned to occasionally pouring salt into her coffee instead of sugar.

“Who is he?” I asked. He opened his mouth, and I held up a finger. “And who are you? And where is home?”

“He is a stranger. I am Dun. Home is in the bunker.”

“A bunker?” A brownie apocalypse prepper. Fantastic.

“So do you want him to go away?”

“Go away?” He blinked twice and his head turtled on his neck. He sounded as horrified by that as by getting something for free.

I focused on his words. He’d said a stranger. Hospitality was a serious thing; welcoming strangers and offering food and drink were key rules of hospitality. Hadn’t I just done the same? Shoving food in his face as soon as I knew he wasn’t a threat? That his guest had then insulted him must be tearing his brain apart.

I caught Juya’s eye across the courtyard. “Any ideas?”

She cackled. The sound was inhuman, like feminine thunder. And said in the same unearthly tones, “You are the Princess.”

I decided to stop asking goddesses for advice. But I knew what my mother would say. I felt a pang at the thought of my mother. She wasn’t saying much of anything at the moment. They called it early-onset Alzheimers. Though no one had ever heard of onset in a single night. I buried that old pain and let my goblin half answer. “Your problem is you think he’s a guest, but he’s a bad guest.”

He nodded so hard he looked about to break his neck.

“Did you invite him?”

He shook his head making his ears flop.

“Then he’s just a squatter. Different thing entirely.”

His eyes lit up. “Different thing entirely? Can it be true?”

“Who’s the Princess?” The word tasted like clay on my tongue.

“You said not to use the P-word.”

“No, I said you couldn’t use the P-word.”

It took a second for him to parse that one. “You can’t come and smite him?”

I shook my head quickly. I was really trying to stop smiting things. “This doesn’t seem like a smiting situation. You can handle it!”

“How can I repay you?” he asked earnestly, flushing to warm mahogany.

“I didn’t do anything!” I protested. I could control the bargain I made, but not the magic that bound me to the terms. It weighed each side according to rules I was still learning. But I knew one of them is that the sides had to be absolutely fair.

He shook his head and held out a hand. He only had four fingers on each. He ticked them off as he said, “You fed me, listened to me, comforted me, and solved my problem!”

“Did not. I only told you how to solve it. That’s not a guarantee it will work?”

His skin paled. “It’s not?”

I sighed. Some of the smaller creatures I’d met had never really figured out deductive reasoning. “Never mind. You are my first brownie! I didn’t even know you existed. It’s more than fair!”

He scratched his chin. “I proved I existed?”

I nodded enthusiastically.

He had to think that one through too, his body cycling through several colors as he did, including his beard which was really disturbing. Then he returned to that rich wooden color as a look of awe washed over his face. “Good bargain.”

A chime sounded in my head, the sign of a bargain fulfilled. The bigger the bargain, the louder the chime, and this one was weirdly loud. Not migraine-inducing and deafening, like the bargain that had started this whole adventure with Scharf, but definitely cathedral bells on a holiday loud. I guess listening to the little dude was more important than I thought. Hunh.

He made an oddly formal bow and scrambled out of the courtyard over the wall. I watched him go with a frown on my face.

A few construction workers trooped into the courtyard, and Osman met them with a tray full of cups of coffee. I could smell the bitter brew from my table. They chatted in Spanish with him and Juya, who handed them various treats without asking what they wanted to eat. They pried the plywood off the empty front window to step inside.

Juya said in her more normal, scratchy “I’m a regular old woman” voice, “Well done.”

I didn’t realize she was saying it to me until she met my eyes and nodded.

I cocked my head. “You’re not mad I broke the guesting laws and told him to be mean?”

She cackled. “Those aren’t my laws. Where I’m from, we bring gifts and only talk politics after the second bottle of wine.”

“Fair enough.” I looked hopefully at her. “Does that deserve cake?”

She looked at the underside of her umbrella with exasperation. “Duende!”

I didn’t stop smiling.

She waved me over and handed over a slice of chocolate cake. I resisted stuffing the entire thing in my mouth and savored a bite of the strong chocolate before saying, “I need something from you if you know it.”

She laughed. “Now you know how I feel.”

I blushed. I guess I did. I was always asking her for help. I knew better than to bargain with her. Just as I could not help the magic of my bargains; Juya could not help the magic of her boons. It went against everything in me, but if I needed something from her, I had to only ask.

I stepped aside so she could serve a couple of students decked out in Howard University gear where Astrid went. We were only a few streets from campus.

When they clattered back to the street, I stepped forward again. “Some people stole a lot of Irish art from the American History Museum.”

She frowned.

“Irish American art,” I added, and her brow cleared. “They were short, pale, dark-haired, wearing dark clothes. They left patterns in the dirt behind. And only I could see them. The people, not the dirt. Everyone could see the dirt.”

She raised a brow.

“No, they weren’t goblins. I don’t think. Were they goblins?”

“How would I know?”

“You haven’t heard anything? Seen anybody new in town? Any rumblings in any of your networks?”

“The skies are tired,” she said with a hint of her more resonant voice. She shook her head and shrunk again. “The city is still recovering from the dragon.”

I peered out from under her umbrella at the pale blue-white sky above. To me, the East Coast skies always looked tired, but Scharf said that was the humidity. “So no.”

She shook her head. “DC is a city of change. There are always new people in town. And also, it never changes. It has not changed now in the way that you mean. But I will keep my eyes perked.”

“Ears.”

“Pardon?”

“You keep your ears perked and your eyes peeled. Never mind. And thank you.”

“The gold warrior approaches.”

I spun around, reaching for my earth magic, getting a metaphorical hold on a few stones in the courtyard, ready to defend Juya when Scharf’s eggplant sedan squealed up to the curb. I looked back at Juya. I forgot sometimes that she didn’t exactly see humans. She saw me as “orange power.” A djinni had been a blue flame. I hadn’t asked her before what she’d seen when she looked at Scharf. A gold warrior. Well then.

The passenger side window opened. “Orange.”

“What?” I’d told her I was stopping by Juya’s and would meet up with her later. Scharf’s definition of later was apparently “now.”

“Come on. We have an interview.” She meant I had an interview. One of the things I could bargain for was the truth from people.

“I thought art crimes was on the case.”

“Lee found the security guard. I asked if I could sit in. Art crimes fobbed him off on Metro PD because he called out.”

I tried to remember Lee’s explanation at the crime scene. One security guard had called out and his replacement was missing. “So this guy wasn’t actually at the museum last night.”

Scharf sagged. “Exactly, but he might be involved.”

“By not being at the museum?”

“Yeah, therefore letting in the real perpetrator.”

I sighed. The one security guard who wasn’t at the museum for the theft didn’t seem to be much of a lead, which was probably why art crimes had punted it. And why Scharf could get in on it.

I looked at Juya, “Can you tell Astrid I stopped by, and I’ll see her later? I won’t flake? Barring. . .” Crime Fighting and business starting were hard to mix, but I was determined to do better.

“Barring monsters in the dark.”

“Barring a magical creature. We aren’t monsters.”

She raised her brow.

“We aren’t.”

She leaned around me and said, “Ahora, si!”

The tourists from a few minutes before had returned and entered the courtyard without questioning why they had walked away and returned. I was happy I hadn’t driven them away.

4

The missing security guard lived at the end of the yellow Metro line in a condo close enough to be rumbled by the overhead tracks. Most of the subway system was underground, but out in the suburbs, they started popping up.

Scharf ducked out of her car and fixed shades over her eyes. She’d traded her blue FBI windbreaker for a tailored blue blazer that barely fit over her sidearm. I crossed my arms in the orange leather jacket Scharf had given me and considered glamouring it into a blazer, but I wouldn’t have been able to pull off the posture or the glare, so gave up that thought. We spotted Lee talking to a cop already parked out front. Scharf waved and he motioned her closer.

“Thanks for calling,” Scharf said.

Lee nodded and turned to the cop without introducing us. “Officer Smythe here was just telling me there was quite a to-do here last night. O’Connor’s kid went missing for a couple of hours.”

Scharf immediately sobered, her face going a shade paler. “Kidnapping?”

Lee raised an eyebrow at Smythe, who had a weaselly face that matched his name. “No, Ma’am. We’ve been called to this residence before.”

“So the child ran away?”

Smythe shrugged. “Kid’s four and not talking yet. Has a history of wandering.”

I frowned. “Not talking yet?” They both looked at me. “That’s late. For talking.”

“And early for running away,” Scharf added.

“So this all may be a waste of time,” Lee said. “The father’s alibi is most of the 405.”

“A child goes missing the night of a heist? I think that’s worth a conversation,” Scharf said quietly.

Lee nodded to Smythe who escorted us to the door and knocked. “Tabitha? We have some detectives here to see you.”

O’Connor opened the door.

He was dressed in a familiar rumpled brown uniform for museum staff. He had hair more orange than mine, which was really saying something, and his face had more freckles than plain skin. His wife joined him seconds later. She looked like a gaunt version of Scharf with dark hair and rings under her eyes. She wore a sweatshirt and a pair of pajama pants covered in red and blue “DCs” for some sports team.

“He wouldn’t wander,” Tabitha said. “Since we moved his bedroom to the second floor, there hasn’t been one incident.”

Her husband rolled his eyes. “So what, someone magically spirited him somewhere? He got downstairs. He got out. He’s back. Everything is fine.”

“Everything is fine?” Lee echoed.

Tabitha clasped her hands together like she was praying. “He wouldn’t leave me, not when I’m here in the house!”

“I know, Tabitha,” Smythe said in a quiet voice. “These folks are here investigating that very fact.”

Scharf glanced at Lee who gave a half shuffle back and gestured to Scharf. I resisted rolling my eyes. In our previous cases, he always passed weeping or hysterical people off to Scharf. It wasn’t because she was an FBI agent.

“Let’s all go inside,” Scharf said in her victim-comforting voice.

The woman retreated immediately, but O’Connor stood staring at us for another half second with a bleak, hostile expression. He took a deep breath. “Fine.”

The living room was tidy except for heaps of games and controllers under the TV in a dark pasteboard stand. O’Connor sat with his wife on a battered sofa.

I saw their son on the floor. He wore a matching blue sweat suit and sat staring at a game on a phone that was chiming at him with a jaunty little melody. I could see both of his parents in his dark hair, green eyes, and freckles. His mother reached toward him but retracted her hand short of touching him.

Scharf crouched down by the arm of the couch, so her head was lower than theirs. “I’m sorry to make you go through this again. Can you tell me what happened?”

I crouched down by the child.

Tabitha stretched an arm toward him. “Don’t distract him.”

All of us looked at the kid engrossed in his game and back at her.

“He doesn’t like to be touched. He’s easily startled. Just leave him be. He’s imagining a different world.”

I looked at the kid who hadn’t moved or acknowledged his mother. “No worries,” I said softly.

O’Connor asked, “Who are you?”

“My name is Laura,” Scharf said quietly. “There was an incident at the museum last night.”

“I wasn’t there last night! I was running around the neighborhood calling for my kid, who never answers.”

His wife petted at him. “He doesn’t understand any of the things you shout at him.”

“Or anything else, which is why he needs testing —”

“Which is why we need to know what happened last night,” Scharf said a little more firmly.

“Well…” Tabitha began.

“The truth of what happened last night. And in exchange,” I thought quickly, trying to come up with a fair bargain. “I’ll talk very softly.”

Tabitha said, “Okay?”

“Mr. O’Connor?” I asked since it was really him we were worried about. “Sounds like a deal?”

“Sure. Nothing happened.”

“Great!” The chime sounded signaling my magic would now ensure both of us kept the bargain, whether we wanted to or not. This was a bargain I was getting good at. Getting the truth out of a suspect with magic was probably half the reason Scharf kept me around. “Can you expand a little on the nothing that happened?”

O’Connor scratched his chin. “I was heading to work. I work the overnights in the security center, nine to five, and Tabitha came down the hall screaming. Even though Caden hates screaming.”

She scooted away from her husband. “He was gone.”

He deflated. “He was gone.”

“This has happened before?” Scharf asked.

Tabitha held her hands out, beseeching. “Only twice. It happens with every child. You look away for two seconds and they’re gone. I don’t know how he got out.”

“And then you called the police?” Scharf asked.

Both Tabitha and Lee nodded.

“He came back on his own!” she said, her hands clasped beneath her chin. “He came back.”

“And you knew nothing about the theft?” Scharf asked.

“As I told the rest of the force, no!” O’Connor said. “No weird people approaching me. Nothing different last night. No hint of anything. I certainly didn’t make off with a bunch of jugs with infinity knots on them!”

I snickered and the kid jumped.

“Sorry!” I said and he jumped again.

“Nothing,” O’Connor repeated.

Scharf looked at me.

I waited for a second, then sighed, I’d forgotten to fulfill my end of the bargain. I said softly, “That must have been terrifying.”

No chime.

I ground my teeth and thought back on exactly what I said about my end of the bargain. Then I whispered very softly, “I’m glad he’s home.”

“What did you say?” O’Connor asked.

“I’m glad he home,” I said louder and with feeling when the chime in my head of a bargain was fulfilled.

They’d told the truth. I had spoken very softly in exchange. I nodded at Scharf; he wasn’t involved. I started to stand up but paused. I’d only gotten their movements. Nobody knew what happened to the kid. “You’re absolutely sure he wasn’t taken?”

O’Connor said, “Yes.”

His wife at the same moment said, “No.”

O’Connor let out a put-upon sigh and said, “The window didn’t magically open.”

I squeaked. Scharf with better control just froze for a second. “Can I see this window?”

His wife jumped up. “It was shut.” She looked at her husband. “You shut it! He always checks all doors and windows before he leaves the house. I joke that we don’t pay him to guard this place. But I like that he does it.”

“I missed one,” he said. “Or you opened it.”

“The window?” Scharf said and Tabitha looked at Caden. She gathered him up on her hip with murmured words, but he didn’t want to go. He squirmed and screeched as her pleas for patience got louder and louder. Inside his room, she set him down immediately and he clutched the phone with both hands, breathing hard.

The room was garish and filled with toys. I cringed at the sight. It didn’t seem right for the quiet boy at our feet.

She pointed to the window. Lee went over but Scharf stayed by me. “What do you smell?”

“I’m not a bloodhound.”

“Metaphorically speaking.”

“It looks like a kid’s room to me.”

Lee turned back to Tabitha. “This has always been missing a screen?”

She nodded. “Ever since Caden…” She glanced at her husband. “He didn’t mean to.” She spun back to us. “And we keep it closed when he’s here. Always. He can’t get out this way.”

I looked inside the windowsill and caught a smudge of dirt. I squinted and pointed to it. As Lee pounced on it, I looked around the room. “Where was Caden the last time you saw him last night?”

She sniffed. “He was in his highchair in the kitchen. That’s why I thought it was safe. I just had to pee. I just left for a second!”

Scharf said, “Show me.”

We trooped out and past the living room into the kitchen in the back half of the condo. Tabitha repeated picking up Caden, murmuring apologies, before she set him down again in the same spot.

In the kitchen, I looked at the highchair, a cheap rickety aluminum thing with a plastic tray. A half-spilled bowl of applesauce sat on the tray. I touched it; it was tacky and dry.

Scharf started to leave, but I shook my head. I’d protested that I had no further way of getting information aside from the bargains I made, but there was one more thing I could try.

I could bargain like any goblin, but I was also a hobgoblin, and that meant I had another source of power—humanity. Hopes, fears, wishes, dreams. I could bargain with emotion and affect humans. It was mostly good for practical jokes. It didn’t have nearly the brute force of my pulling-down-buildings abilities, but occasionally it revealed things that others would rather keep hidden. The other beautiful thing was that I was also half human, which meant unlike most pure-bred goblins, I could bargain with myself. My human half got something, and my goblin half got something. It was the quintessential win-win.

But it was also surprisingly exhausting, and why bother when I had full-blooded humans around to bargain with. I turned to Scharf and said, “You go the speed limit the entire way home, and I will find out what happened here.”

She huffed out a laugh before schooling her features. “Deal.”

I closed my eyes at the chime and then waited. The downside to hobbing was that it wasn’t something I could direct and control. I was as much at the mercy of these bargains as anyone else. When nothing happened, I opened my eyes. Scharf now had both eyebrows raised.

Lee was looking between us. “What are you talking about?”

I winced, forgetting I had not been invisible. “Nothing.”

“What is going on?” he asked.

I stalked to the back sliding glass doors to get away from him. I huffed out a breath and then froze at the curve of leaf that was revealed with my breath.

I heard Scharf mutter curse words under her breath. “Let’s go.”

I took a deep breath and let it out, breathing hot air over the still-cool glass door. A bigger pattern emerged like streaks in a mirror after a shower.

I breathed again. Three loops intersected like a traditional Celtic infinity knot, but on the outer edge of each knot, an additional two points stretched to touch each other. Six points all together formed a circle around the central three knots. “Scharf?”

“What exactly is she consulting about?” Lee asked.

“Guys!” I breathed again to keep it fresh and pointed.

She looked closer and whipped out her phone to take a picture. Lee leaned over my shoulder to see. “Would you look at that?”

He glanced at the uniformed officer who’d trailed us around the room. “Lock it down. He was taken.”

“What? He’s right there.”

The kid had followed us to the kitchen and stood stock still in the door, phone forgotten in his hand. He pointed right at me.

Everyone else looked at me too.

I crouched down in front of him. “What, kid?”

He kept pointing until his finger touched my ears. I realized one tip was peeking through my curls and gasped. Through my glamour, he wasn’t supposed to see the weird tips of my ears, pointed and ragged instead of round like somebody had gnawed on an elf’s ear. Blocking the view from the crowd, I pulled my hair away and let the floppy end of my right ear extend.

His eyes got round in his face.

“They looked like me?” I whispered. “The ones who grabbed you? He looked like me with my ears?”

The guys on the video had been wearing hats.

He pointed until his finger touched one extended flap, and we both flinched. I tucked the extra skin away, and his gaze stopped tracking me and drifted off into a corner of the room.

I twisted and looked at Scharf, who was the only one now looking at me and not the kid. “They took him. And they had my ears.”

Lee asked, “What ears?”

She took a breath. “But your source didn’t know what they were?”

“What they were?” Lee asked.

I shook my head.

“They kidnapped a child, “ Scharf said.

I nodded.

<<<>>>

“You want me to talk at the 7’o’clock.”

I watched a subway train thirty feet above the ground pull into the Fort Totten station and heard the announcer informing everyone that they had arrived and how to transfer lines on the lower level. We’d walked away from the collection of cops and additional agents that had shown up once Lee had reported his findings. “You want me to show them the little men I saw on the tape.”

Scharf rocked and said nothing.

“And tell them how I could see them when they couldn’t.”

She scratched the side of her head. “I really don’t want you to. But I need you to.”

“They brought him back.” I stopped, realizing what I just said. “They brought him back. A changeling. My god, we’re living out some kind of twisted fairy tale!”

“What?”

“Changeling children. An old Celtic myth. Be careful not to upset the fae folk because they’ll steal your kids and leave a changeling in their place. It has a pretty horrible history.”

She tugged on her hair with both hands. “You think?”

“Parents got rid of changeling children. What historians think this was really about was getting rid of any kids who were different, who they didn’t think would contribute. You don’t feel so guilty if it’s not really your kid. I didn’t think there were actual fairies involved, just humans being horrible like usual.”

“Or desperate like usual.”

I thought of the wild look in O’Connor’s eyes and nodded. “Why would the fairies want O’Connor out of the museum?”

Scharf gave a one-shouldered shrug.

“You were right. So they could replace him.”

She nodded once. “They still haven’t found the replacement who worked instead of O’Connor.”

I scratched my head and pulled my hand away, realizing I was mimicking Scharf. “There are stories of adults who go missing in fairyland too. Though they don’t usually leave a changeling behind.”

“Fairyland?”

“Underhill. Elysium. Never Never Land. Avalon. Camelot. The Seelie and Unseelie Courts.” I’d spent a lot of time in various worlds for my anthropology degree. “Though in Ireland, it’d be Tír na nÓg.”

“Is this an actual place I can go?”

“And arrest actual people? No.” I scuffed a toe in the dirt. “I don’t think so.”

She looked interested. “Maybe?”

“The only direction I have is that you get there on a magic horse.”

“Of course.”

“It’s an island. With orchids. And salmon.” I dug through my memory. “And only women live there.”

She snorted. “Well, I’d like that part.”

I laughed. “Men can visit.”

“Darn.” Scharf’s ex-girlfriend followed her to DC when she’d been posted here and then dumped her. I hadn’t seen Scharf dating anyone else since then, so an island full of women probably sounded great at this point.

“Sorry. Unless you know a giant salmon we can ride, we can’t go looking for lost security guards in Tír na nÓg. And I can’t believe I even said that out loud.”

“So you’ll do it?”

“The seven o’clock? Let’s play that one out, shall we? You’re going to march into the meeting, provided the head dude even shows up, and tell him what? My pet goblin told me changelings aren’t fiction?”

“Folklore.”

She stole my usual line, one that I used to bug her: the difference between myth, folklore, and fiction. (Large amounts of time and repetition.) But it did give me an idea. “How about I go, but as a folklore expert?”

“The point is to tell them it isn’t folklore,” she said mildly. She was using her interrogation voice on me, and I was trying and failing not to let it get to me.

“You know they’re on the tape!” I whined. “You’ll notice them. You can tell them.”

She shrugged and I didn’t know if that was agreement or violent disagreement.

I plowed ahead. “And then I talk about things, people, whatever, that can go unnoticed. Historically speaking. From folklore.”

“What if they don’t notice them?”

I bit my lip. “I’ve reached the end of my planning abilities. We’ll take it from there. What were you going to do at the task force before kids started going missing?”

“Say as little as possible and see who else is coming.”

A chill went down my spine. “Who else is coming?”

“I don’t know.”

“Scharf. You can’t walk into a conference room filled with who knows what kind of creature with who knows what agenda! Now, I have to come!”

She snorted and headed back to her sedan. “You’re concern for my safety is touching.”

I crossed my arms for a second but she didn’t look back, so I dropped my arms and trudged after her. “It was more access to your couch I was worried about.”

“I heard that.”

“If you get eaten by a troll, where will I sleep?”

“Have you met a troll?”

“Not yet, but with my luck, it’s only a matter of time.” I plopped into the passenger seat and buckled up quickly as she got in. “But I think it’s in our best interest to pretend that all of these creatures are real and may want to kill us.”

“Or kidnap a child.”

She peeled out and I grit my teeth and hung on. “Or that.”

5

I took a deep breath and let it out as I craned my neck to take in the Hoover Building. Despite agreeing to this, I could not force myself to turn visible as I waited for Scharf in the early morning light. Despite my many investigations, I’d never been inside this building.

Scharf was assigned to a DC field office, which didn’t have anything more to do with the national FBI headquarters than any other field office in any other city. She worked with a collection of agents on the Metro area’s crime and didn’t worry about running the whole FBI.

It was not a pretty building like the monuments only blocks away. It looked like it had been built in the seventies when the aesthetic had tried for modern and came out industrial. Square windows were framed by bare cement walls. The ground floor was sealed, and the offices didn’t start until two floors up. Strategic cement planters stacked up around the entrance, presumably in case of ramming. The entire building screamed paranoia and defense. Hoover was a good name for it.

“Princess!”

I spun around and adjusted my gaze two feet down.

I sighed. Another goblin, purple this time, stood between two planters with his hands clasped beneath his chin.

“I cannot help you right now,” I told him.

“But, but, but —”

“Not now!” I made a shooing gesture at the goblin, and he finally walked dejectedly right into traffic. I sucked in a breath, but without looking, he avoided the cars zipping by even this early in the morning.

Another voice called, “Princess!”

I spun, ready to dispatch another goblin, and waved in relief when I saw the next goblin approaching me was Mrs. Henderson, Scharf’s office secretary. I was fairly sure she’d wait until doomsday before asking for my help.

We hadn’t gotten off on the right foot, mostly because I was royalty and had no idea what I was doing. She thought she could run the world much more efficiently; she was probably right.

I tensed; could I be sure of that? “Please tell me you don’t want help with unwanted guests or unsightly hair or whatever I’m supposed to be helping these people with.”

“Are all the little beasts coming begging?” Mrs. Henderson said with a grin of sharpened teeth.

I winced at the name. Goblins were beasts in the magical world, but that word didn’t seem to offend anybody but me. I shifted and said, “Yeah.”

“You had your chance to duck this, and you jumped in with both feet, so don’t come crying to me.” Her usual gold bangles clanked as she crossed her arms. She wore an olive-green jumpsuit from some house of fashion with black, high-heeled ankle boots.

The jumpsuit’s color was a joke because it was the precise color of her real skin. I knew that, to everyone else, she looked like a little person with brown skin, black hair, and normal teeth, ears, and nails. Just like I looked blond and tanned to casual observers instead of orange. Fortunately, I got my mother’s teeth and they looked normal whether I was hiding or not.

In contrast, I’d attempted to find the most professional things in my wardrobe, but they just screamed thrift store. When you burn through clothes—literally—fashion is not a high priority. I wore nice dark jeans and a red button-down shirt that wasn’t too, too big. Though I could see in the increasing light that it clashed with my orange leather jacket.

She clicked her nails against her teeth; they made a louder clack than they should. “What are you doing here?”

I didn’t know the topic of the meeting. “The seven o’clock?”

Her face turned a lighter shade of green, a Castelvetrano fresh olive instead of her normal dark pickled-olive color. Her skin was less changeable than the brownie’s, which meant she was genuinely upset. “You’re coming to the NTEPEUL task force meeting. You’re the hot recruit?”

I was still trying to decide whether to be flattered or terrified by the description. “How do you know about NTEPUEL?”

“You honestly think I’d let some bigwigs start a task force about us and not get myself assigned to it?” She had a real antipathy to me, but in general, she was one of the good ones. She helped cover up any crimes or problems the magical world got itself into it as far as one goblin could. “What are you thinking?”

Scharf said, “You ready?”

I jumped and said, “Eep.”

I hadn’t seen her approach. She looked me up and down, and I did the same to her. She was in a snazzier black suit than usual, a button-down white shirt, and her usual sunglasses tucked into her dark hair. Her badge was clipped visibly to her belt.
