He sobered. “There was a rape at knifepoint, three robberies, and an attempted homicide in the past forty-eight hours. And I’m on a task force looking for dirt.”

“So it’s cool to steal millennia of history?” I asked.

“It’s not millennia,” Cool butted in, and I pivoted to see the rest of the task force approaching the elevators. “The oldest thing in there was 300 years. It’s all American fabricated art.” I could hear the air quotes around art.

“So it’s inferior?” Scharf asked.

“I didn’t say that.”

I remembered Kristy’s devastated eyes at the loss of her grandfather’s art. “I saw some of it. The knives were gorgeous.”

“You saw some of it?” Carlisle asked. “Why didn’t you say anything?”

“Not what was stolen. What an artist didn’t give to the Smithsonian.”

“Ooh, where is that art?” The psychic asked with moony eyes.

“Celtic Central,” I said, reluctantly. “Off Dupont, ironically. The artist’s granddaughter works there. They’re really cool, is all I’m saying.” I looked at Lee. “But obviously, keeping people safe is, uh, cooler.”

Carlisle sniffed. “I’m with Lee. I will do my job, though why we think a weird, spliced video and some dirt is an unexplained crime, I have no idea.”

I winced. This task force wasn’t even remotely a secret.

10

Scharf kicked at the bare patch of dirt she’d dug up and said, “This isn’t it.”

Hernandez carefully stuck a scoop of dirt into a test tube and capped it. She stowed the sample in her bag and shrugged. “We have to run the test.”

“This isn’t it.” Scharf raised a brow. “Is it?”

Hernandez slumped as she ran a finger through the dirt. “No, probably not. I mean, I cast a wide net because I didn’t want to be wrong and screw up an investigation, but. . .”

“The crime scene is in Virginia,” Scharf finished for her.

Hernandez squatted down to examine another patch of grass. “I won’t say that for sure, but somewhere a lot closer to the river, given diatoms that are only native north of Virginia. But that actually pointed to a possible Maryland site as well. They were Maryland diatoms.”

I raised my hand. “What’s a diatom?”

“Algae. Microscopic algae. Used to identify bodies of water. It’s actually like a fingerprint. You see—”

“Got it. Thank you.”

“It could have been here,” Hernandez said plaintively.

“This isn’t it,” Scharf repeated.

Hernandez spun in a circle. “Where’s the fort? Maybe I could look closer.”

Scharf laughed but there was no humor in her voice. “This wasn’t a fort. This was a battlefield.”

“I’ve never heard of it.” I did my own circle and found the brown wooden National Park Service sign that said Antietam National Battlefield. A visitor’s center, a fence, and a long stretch of lawn down to a small white building were the only things in sight. “It doesn’t look that big.”

“It wasn’t,” Scharf confirmed. “The North and the South ran into each other for twelve hours and died over a few miles.”

“Seriously. I’ve never heard of this!” I said. “ I mean, we only spent like a month on the Civil War.” I turned to Hernandez. “I grew up in California. We spent more time on the gold rush.”

Hernandez nodded. “I grew up in Pennsylvania. It’s not that far away, and I didn’t know either. I learned everything possible about Gettysburg. You’d think that was the only thing that happened in the war.”

Scharf smiled. “I didn’t learn about this in school either. I learned about this place in officer training in the army. It’s the single bloodiest day of war in American history.”

I looked around in horror. “Even D-Day? Pearl Harbor?”

“It helped the numbers immensely that both sides were American. Really racked up the casualties.”

I caught the spooky nature of the place. Thousands dead on a tiny stretch of a few fields and woods was unfathomable to me. It looks so peaceful now, dried fields showing the first hints of spring.

“Also, it included one of the biggest mistakes of military command. An officer was supposed to reinforce a line but instead yelled, ‘About face.’ To this day, nobody knows why. Five Confederate regiments retreated instead of engaging. Who knows? If Lee had succeeded, we might be standing in a different country right now.”

I turned back to her. “General Lee was involved here?”

Scharf hid her smile. “He led the entire Confederate army. His fingerprints will be on every piece of land we visit.”

I shuddered. In California, the Civil War was ancient history lost amidst the sprint through the various wars that make up “social studies.” Out here, it still scarred the land. Literally.

Scharf’s phone rang, and she walked away to take it. “You’re done already?” she asked the phone.

I wandered to one of the signs stuck into the ground on the side of the path and read about the people who died in the North Wood as opposed to Bloody Lane.

I saw something moving in the dead grass. “What the. . .”

When a head popped up, the same color as the golden stalks with ears flopping, my eyes rounded. I spun back to Hernandez. “Why don’t you head back to the vehicle? We have to get to the next site.”

She smiled. “Field work is kind of fun. I never get out.”

I turned back. The goblin was forty feet closer in seconds. I turned back and stepped in front of the field instinctively shielding him. “Great, then can you talk to the ranger on duty? Make sure they haven’t seen anything. Any digging?”

She paled and gulped. “Okay!”

As she hiked toward the visitor center near the parking lot, I spun back around.

“Not here. Are you crazy? I’m not going to get you a girlfriend or kick out a house guest or whatever you want.”

He cocked his head. He was the smallest goblin I’d ever seen. Barely two feet tall. I hadn’t mistaken his color, like ripe wheat, but his hair was white and there was only a tuft left of it on top of his head. There was more of it coming out of both ears. He was dressed in a tunic of rough burlap that matched his skin, and his feet looked huge for his body, bare even in the March chill.

“Well?” I crossed my arms.

“Hello,” he said in a voice far too deep for his body.

“Hi.”

“Welcome guest. What can I do for ye?”

My arms fell to my sides. “You live here.”

He pointed behind him without looking at the white cottage-type building.

“You live there?” I asked.

He laughed, coughed, and shook his head. He pointed up, and beyond the woods, I could see the top of a much larger building.

“That is my home,” he said. Which means something different to goblins. He was bonded to this place. I couldn’t imagine it.

“Nice home. What do you want?”

“Not your line. My line. You’re a guest.”

“What do I want?”

He nodded.

“Do you know who I am?” I cringed as I said it. As if I was some crazy bigwig.

“A guest!” He cocked his head and blinked eyes far too big for his wrinkled face. His irises were the same gold as the rest of him. He looked to be a thousand years old. Maybe he was.

“You don’t want anything from me?” I asked hesitantly.

“Come for tea?”

Was this the start of a bargain? “Um, no thanks. I can’t. I’m sorry.” I was surprised to realize I actually was sorry. He apparently wasn’t after anything; he just lived here. “Actually, can I ask you a question?”

“Your question proves that you can.”

I chuckled. “May I?”

“That question also proves that you may.”

I clenched my teeth, but he was right. “Fair. Do you know what happened here? The battle?” That question came out of my mouth with no control from me. I had meant to ask about anybody hiding large amounts of art or holes in the ground.

He nodded. “My home has become a museum to the battle. All I know is what happened here.”

Scharf tramped back across the field. “Lee didn’t find anything, Detective Lee that is, not the General. Let’s head out.” As she reached me, she looked around. “Where’s Hernandez?”

I pointed to the ranger station behind me and said, “So you were here before the battle.”

“This is my home.”

Scharf looked around and froze. “Who’s there?”

I realized he must be invisible as Scharf kept pivoting, a hand on her Glock.

“Will you show my friend?” I asked.

He looked between me and Scharf. “She is human.”

“Yeah, but she’s okay.”

“What is there?” Scharf asked carefully, slowing to look in the same direction I was.

“What’s your name?” I asked.

He blinked and said, “Richard. That is my most recent name. I forget the others.”

Scharf jumped, and her eyes dropped three feet to focus on Richard. I didn’t see anything different, but he must have revealed himself to her.

Scharf crouched down. “Nice to meet you, Richard?”

He just blinked.

“Do you want us to call you Richard?” I asked.

“What else would you call me?” he replied, his head cocked. He had none of the frenetic energy of other goblins I knew, including myself.

I shrugged. “Whatever you want.”

“Whatever is an odd name. I like it. I shall be Whatever.”

Scharf shook her head. “Goblins, man.”

“You know of the beasts?” he asked.

“Don’t call us that,” I said reflexively.

“You shall rename our very beings? I do not know who you are that you have such power.”

“I’m the Peallaidh.”

“Are not.” He smiled and sat on the ground, which made Scharf sit on the ground too. I sunk down, feeling suddenly fifty feet tall. He continued, “Name’s taken. No one is Whatever, so I can be. Someone is Peallaidh.” He pronounced it correctly.

“You knew my father?”

“Ah, past tense. Sorry.” He reached out and petted me with a tiny, dry hand.

“Yeah, I didn’t name myself. I inherited that one.” I traced a finger in the dry dirt. “Uh, when did you know him?”

“Came out this way a hundred years ago. He liked this part of the world. Lots of striving people, lots of emotions to harvest.”

Scharf slanted her eyes to me. “What does that mean?”

The goblin, whose name was now apparently Whatever, looked between us. “Knows us and does not know us. I have never encountered a human like this.”

I sat on the ground. “Me neither.”

“I feel strange without refreshment between us. I’m breaking the rules of guesting. I have not had a guest for many years. Your father visited me sometimes. But there is not enough striving here. Only mourning, but I am content to mourn also.”

“Sorry,” I repeated, though I wasn’t sure what I was sorry for. The entire Civil War? Humanity?

“There is less mourning now. Just remembering. Which is better.”

“Has there been anybody new around? Maybe with a number of artifacts?” Scharf asked the question I hadn’t.

I felt like I needed to offer a bargain, but he’d repeatedly said he wanted nothing from me. He seemed beyond bargains. Maybe after long enough, he’d done enough.

“No change. Is our quiet time. Times of change are the fall anniversary, the winter candles, and the summer for visitors. This is the in-between time.”

I frowned. “Candles?”

“They light a candle for everyone who died. Every December,” Scharf said quietly.

“How… festive,” I said.

“Anybody deposit a large amount of stuff somewhere on the land?” Scharf asked. “Or take about fifty pounds of dirt with them?”

“This place is unchanged. Is why I stay. I am too old for change. I would rather remember.”

“Thank you, Whatever,” I said when Scharf didn’t have another question.

“You have given me my name. But I have given you nothing.” He seemed distressed by this.

I racked my brain for a second. I felt guilty for disturbing his peace. “You gave me a memory of my father, a worthy gift.”

“You do not have memories of your own?”

I shook my head.

“And yet you are the Peallaidh?”

“Yeah, his final joke.”

He cocked his head and touched my knee again with one finger. “And yet you are sad, not laughing.”

I frowned, realizing he hadn’t guessed that; he felt that. “You’re a hobgoblin.”

“You did not rename our species!” He looked happy to hear it.

I had never met one before, well, besides my father and myself. Most goblins, like Mrs. Henderson and the others I’d met, are just goblins. Their power lies only in the base elements like earth, water, and fire. Hobgoblins can also deal in emotion, a power usually reserved for much more powerful spirits like my demon boyfriend.

“I thought you’d be taller,” I said, surprised again by the questions popping out of my mouth.

He cocked his head. “Why?”

I shook my head. “I… I don’t know. My father was. I am.”

“He was not content to only remember. Even so old, he was still trying to massage the world. Is why he is—was—on top.”

I leaned back on my hands, a bit stunned. “Is that what it means to be a leader of goblins? The one who wants to, uh, massage the world the most?”

He tipped over with a peel of laughter. “The one who leads the world makes all goblins his guest.” He tipped his chin to me. “Or hers.”

It was the single best explanation I had ever received of my title and the only one that made sense to me.

All the goblins I’d met had insisted my “title” was about power. But instead, I made them my guest, made them welcome in my life, and tried to make their lives better. I’d remember that one.

He climbed to his feet laboriously. We lived long lives, but I did not think, seeing him, that they were immortal lives.

“You’re sure I can’t do anything more for you?” he asked.

I cocked my head. “Why not pull a trick? Bargain? Ask for something?”

“Bargains are to see who is worthy of being a guest. But anyone who pays tribute here is already worthy. They sacrificed for us all enough.”

Scharf smiled quietly. “I like that.”

My mind was blown again. Is that why we bargained and why we teased? Not for power but for trust? I was beginning to question everything I knew about myself.

He blinked at Scharf. “Humans trust without a bargain, only with trust. Then when betrayed, you fight. Strange species.”

Scharf bowed a little to him before looking out over the battlefield. “Yes. Very, very strange.”

“What about a quest?” I asked abruptly, remembering the two-toned chime in Jack’s apartment when the little goblin had asked me for a girlfriend.

He nodded solemnly. “A quest is for when someone must trust themselves.”

“But we can set it for them?”

He cocked his head. “Of course?”

“And they bargain with themselves?”

He laughed, a pure goblin cackle that made him sound younger. “Clever way with words this one.”

Scharf laughed. “You could say that.”

“Come to visit in another century. Then have tea.”

“I will do that,” I said. We weren’t bargaining, but I heard the chime all the same. I took a deep breath. I had an obligation to fulfill in a century. I didn’t know if I would even be able to. My father lived for centuries, but I didn’t know what effect my human half would have on all of that.

Scharf looked between us. “Century? One hundred years century? I’m afraid it will have to be a little sooner than that for me.”

Whatever tugged on his remaining tuft of hair. “Of course. Come whenever.” He giggled again. “Come visit Whatever whenever!”

Hernandez emerged from the visitor log and Whatever blinked. “Is another human?”

“No!” Scharf and I said quickly.

He bowed and disappeared.

“Centuries?” Scharf said as soon as he was gone.

“Probably. I don’t really know.”

“He was here for the battle. Not a reenactment. He was present for the battle of Antietam.”

Hernandez came bounding up. “The ranger said that they are starting their spring gardening beds, and they got pretty churned up.”

“This isn’t it,” Scharf repeated, looking off into the field.

“Oh, okay.” Hernandez deflated.

“Thanks though!” I said quickly. “We found a local to talk to who already checked out the flower beds.”

As we hiked back to her car, Scharf whispered, “What else don’t I know?”

“What else don’t I know?” I whispered back.

11

“So we’re calling them magic.” I surveyed the motley collection of baggies of ingredients spread out on the plastic table under a square white tent. “Magic mug cakes.”

It was the first day of our stall in the farmer’s market at Dupont Circle. Cars whizzed by on the main roundabout just a street away even this early in the morning, but the market was quiet with vendors unpacking under the white square canopies.

I wondered why my life started at 6 AM every day now. Scharf, Hernandez, and I had circled through a half dozen historical sites sampling dirt, but as Scharf had declared, nothing in Maryland fit, and no other goblins popped out of the bushes. We’d called it when the sun set. I’d bailed out of today’s Civil War reenactment at five more battlefields. Though as I surveyed our crooked picnic table, I wished I hadn’t.

Astrid, who was currently taping a piece of poster board to the front of the table, had spent the week securing permits and shelling out the cash from all our previous sales, a loan from her aunt, and everything I made at my last cleaning job for the rental fee for the season. I’d spent the last twenty-four hours mixing up a storm. One baggy with oil, vinegar, and water. One baggy with flour, baking powder, sugar, and various goodies.

I held pages of address labels printed with the ingredients, a prominent “stamp” that said vegan, and Magic Mug Cakes in a swirly font. I’d shelled out an extra five dollars for vegan sugar at a fancy health food store after Astrid came to me with a dozen printouts about bone char in regular sugar, which is a thing I did not need to know. I hoped it would be worth the extra expense.

Astrid was dressed in a suit. A legitimate black suit. At least she had a red T-shirt on underneath the jacket. It matched the red on the sign in front of the stall. She’d straightened her hair, and the coarse black locks looped artfully around her head. Her dark skin was covered with a layer of powder that matched it exactly, and her eyes were fully made up, though her lips were bare. I felt grubby in comparison.

“Yeah. Magic.” Astrid stood up. “We have to call it what people expect them to be.”

“Magic?”

“That’s what you called them.”

“When?” I asked, flabbergasted.

She grinned. “The first time I talked about trying to turn them into an actual business. You called them magic cakes. Or maybe it was Juya. It’s perfect.”

“I didn’t think we had decided on the name of the business.”

“We’re not calling them Orange and Astrid’s Cakes in a Bag. Leave the branding to me.”

I knew she wasn’t talking about burning metal horseshoes in the side of cows, but I didn’t have a real clear picture of what branding entailed. “Magic Cake? Ironic.”

“Why ironic? Because they’re not magic? That’s not really what irony is.”

I had a fabulous idea of what irony is. “What if we start making cookies?”

“Great point.” She started to pace. She wasn’t quite as restless as Scharf, who was allergic to furniture, but she got antsy after a few minutes of not accomplishing things. Both of them exhausted me. “We should leave more room for expansion of the brand. We could be on supermarket shelves and confusing people.”

I laughed loud and hard. “We’re not going to be on supermarket shelves.”

She stopped. “We could. How about Magic Bakes? You don’t think you’ll expand past baking, will you?”

I fought another guffaw. “It sounds like a strain of weed.”

“What?”

“Magic Bakes, you know, like a marijuana strain. Maybe that’s the business we should be in.”

“With this skin? Honey, I ain’t touching a joint until god himself changes federal law.”

“Fair enough. Or actually, not at all fair. Sorry. I don’t smoke because…” Even before I knew I was magic, I didn’t like being out of control. Was it a subconscious thought that I might turn invisible if I got high? “Actually though, we could get into the munchies crowd. I think it got legalized here.”

“No way. The healthy junk food market is way bigger.”

“So all of California.”

“Exactly. That state.” She said “that state” like it was a foreign country where we didn’t have an extradition treaty.

“This is not health food,” I said, and her eyes narrowed in frustration. “But it’s a lovely name.”

And it was personal, though no one would know that. Maybe just saying magic would mean some of mine would rub off, and people would like them more.

She examined our table. “I’m going to have to change all these labels.”

She grabbed for a pouch of office supplies from her backpack, and I held up a hand. “How about we do that for next week? Nobody is going to remember a C versus a B.”

She dug out her phone and walked around the table to snap a picture.

“What are you doing?”

“Commemorating the moment! One day when we’re a big company, we’ll want proof that we once called it something else.”

I forced a smile, though I felt sick. Since the first moment we met in Juya’s courtyard, I had been struck by her optimism. But the further down this path we walked, the more it scared the hell out of me.

She pulled out another bit of poster board and a permanent marker and said, “So we need to be at $5 to recoup our initial investment.”

I gulped and sat on the curb at the back of the little tent. “How about we start at $3? Just for today, to see how they go.”

She cocked her head, considering this, and then shook it. “I don’t have that many ones to give change. Plus, if we’re going to make back the cost of the booth by the end of the season, we need to be charging at least $4.57. And I definitely don’t have the change for that.”

I nodded; I felt like I wasn’t quite attached to the earth. “$5 it is.”

She leaned over and gripped my forearm clutching my knees. “They’re worth $5.”

She pivoted and dug a big metal box out of a backpack. I got a good look at the box and gasped. “You brought a microwave?”

“Well, they won’t buy without a sample! And some might want to eat it here. I checked; we have a power drop.” She pointed to my ankles, and I saw a snaking extension cord in the gutter beneath my feet.

I had to rock back to get my feet out of the way when she grabbed the cord. I caught myself with magic so I didn’t topple into a tree cut into the sidewalk behind me.

You have to get up and help, I told myself, though it took a minute to unclench my hands from around my legs and unfold. “What can I do?”

She plugged in the microwave and stood up. “Start mixing up some samples. It starts in…” She dug her phone out of her back pocket to check the time. “Okay, maybe not yet. It officially opens in twenty minutes.”

I started mixing anyway in the green, biodegradable coffee cups I’d brought. I’d contributed something at least. I hoped they wouldn’t melt in the microwave. I glanced around the rest of the stalls.

In the early blue light, a row of white tents spanned either side of the closed street. Many were empty, but a third of the stalls were filled with fruits and vegetables, pottery, and honey. The one right next to us was occupied, though I couldn’t see what the girl was laying out on the table. As if she could sense my gaze, she turned to look at me and waved.

I waved back and realized I recognized her. “Kristy?”

“I didn’t know you were on the circuit!”

I didn’t know what she was talking about. “I, um, well, we’re here. Kristy, this is Astrid.”

Astrid waved. She was counting cash in an envelope hidden in her Howard University gym bag. “If more than four people pay with anything other than a $5, we won’t have enough change.”

I pointed to the ATM someone was rolling down the street. “We’ll just tell them to go there.”

She brightened and then turned to Kristy. “Sorry. Hi!”

“Hi! I just want to thank you again for all the work you’re doing on the robbery.”

Astrid’s hands fumbled. “Robbery?”

I looked between the two of them and said quietly, “Board game. Cops and Robbers. I met her at a board game night.”

I dashed the ten feet between the two tables and said, “You’re welcome.”

“Any news?”

“Um.” I considered the gunpowder and battlefield searches but thought that might come off wrong. What was Scharf always saying? “We have some leads.”

I looked at the wire knots spread out on a green tablecloth. We needed a tablecloth.

Then I bent to get a closer look. They looked like cheaper versions of the silver jewelry I saw in the shop. I picked up one Celtic infinity knot, and though it was dull wire, the loops were intricate and perfectly even. “This is beautiful.”

Kristy blushed, her fair skin making it obvious. For the millionth time, I thanked my glamour. She picked up a stack of straight wires next to her. “I’m nowhere as good as my Grandda, but I do love it. We sell some out of the shop.” She pointed behind her to the closed sign on the door to the Celtic Central shop. “But I saw all these people wandering in the market and never coming in the door, so I thought, we can do that.”

I grinned and gestured to our stall. “We thought pretty much the same. It’s good to see you.”

She nodded and sat down in front of a cash box. We really needed one of those too. She picked up a pair of little pliers and a length of metal. I was about to leave but froze when I saw a little broach. It was a circle with three loops in the middle and six points around the edge. I’d seen it before in steam on the door of a kidnapped child. I picked it up carefully. “What is this?”

“A pin.”

“No, where did you get the design? Is it a specific design?”

She shrugged. “I mean, there are meanings to everything. That’s a shield. A warrior’s shield.”

I was digging for my phone when Kristy said with flaming cheeks, “I’m sorry, I don’t allow pictures of my work. That was actually my Grandda’s rule.”

“No, I wasn’t… I was just going to… I was going to look it up. Sorry.”

I retreated to my stall and tried to describe the knot, but without a picture, I couldn’t really find anything.

Then all thoughts of the investigation went out of my head when a guy in sweats with his arm looped through his golden retriever’s leash wandered up to the table.

Astrid deftly set out a gooey bit of chocolate cake sample.

“What are these?” he asked as he picked one up.

“Cake,” I said stupidly.

“Mug cakes,” Astrid said in her polite voice. “But you don’t even have to worry about water levels. The liquid is in this separate baggie here.”

He glanced at her. “Weird.”

I slumped but tried to keep my nerves off my face. We’d been selling them for months at Juya’s, but she was a goddess. If someone didn’t like them, Juya could just magic their silence or wave a finger and expel them from her courtyard. I suppose I could hob and do the same, but I didn’t want to succeed because I’d whammied them. I wanted to succeed based on the most common bargain on earth—I gave them a product and they gave me money and we were both happy, no magic involved, whatever Astrid wanted to call the company. I tried not to laugh that she was calling it a company at all.

He tipped the sample into his mouth and said, “Oh my god.”

My shoulders lifted to my ears, and I tried to force them down, but they wouldn’t go.

“That is so good.” He looked at the various baggies with more interest.

“Five dollars,” Astrid said quickly. “That way you can have freshly baked cake whenever you want.”

He looked at a peanut butter chocolate instead of the pure chocolate he sampled. I cringed when he picked it up. I added the peanut butter to the liquid bag, and it made it look like poop. Literally. We needed opaque bags. But he didn’t balk. He fished out a fiver and said, “Thanks.”

He wandered to speak to Kristy for a second before jogging off, his dog pulling him along. Astrid perched in the rickety folding chair the market had provided next to me in its twin. “And I didn’t even have to make change.”

“Never go shopping when you’re hungry,” I said. “That’s the moral of this story.”

“No, we’re in the food business. We want everyone shopping when they’re hungry. They’re great! And there isn’t a single product on the market that has the liquid premeasured for people.”

“Because even the fast-food industry could not believe anyone would be this lazy.”

She shrugged. “I’m telling you; this is going to be big.”

I laughed and finally uncranked my shoulders from my ears. “You bring the faith; I’ll bring the fear.”

“Trust me, if you make enough to walk your dog near Dupont Circle at 6 AM, you can shell out five bucks for breakfast,” she said with a cynicism that reassured me.

I picked up one package. From the front, it didn’t look completely unprofessional. “The labels are really great. Thank you.”

“It was a good idea to say bake instead of cake. There aren’t any mug cookies either. We’re going to dominate the market share.”

I tried to imagine it. “I don’t think that would work. Maybe plate cookies?”

She perked up and dug out a notebook.

“Please don’t write that down.”

“Please work on that.”

“Hello?”

I glanced up and my jaw dropped to see the psychic, Madame Somebody, browsing with her hands outstretched over the merchandise. When she saw me, she smiled and repeated, “Hello.”

“Uh, hi, what are you doing here?” I asked.

She smiled. “I am at the farmer’s market?”

“Astrid, this is Madame... Madame… We, um… That is to say, she’s a psychic.”

Astrid glanced over at me but donned her customer smile. “How nice. I have an aunt who does Tarot, so I’m all good.”

“One does not have to—” the psychic began.

“Have a cake!” I said, thrusting a sample at her.

“They’re vegan!” Astrid said, assuming probably correctly, that it would be the biggest selling point for a psychic.

She glanced down at the label. “And what magic will this cake do?”

I cackled and almost swallowed my tongue trying to stop. “Who said anything about magic?”

The psychic put a single finger on the label of one of the bags.

“That’s the name of our company!” Astrid said.

I crossed my arms. “They appease hunger. That’s the magic.”

“That’s it?”

“That’s it.” I squinted at her. Why did I get the feeling that she knew I could do more? “What other magic could they do?”

“No, thank you,” the psychic said and put down the sample.

“Have a great day then,” Astrid said in an extremely chipper voice. She held a little cup up to a passing couple. “Sample?”

The psychic bypassed Kristy and made her way to Celtic Central. I remembered her interest in the knives they sold. I craned my neck to watch her go through the door, vaguely nervous about her wandering around unsupervised.

When nothing happened, I sighed and turned back. She’d probably just give a knife a Tarot reading.

Astrid looked at me. “A psychic? You didn’t seem like the woo type.”

I couldn’t help it. I lost it again, nearly falling off my chair as I wrapped my hands around my middle to try to stop laughing.

Kristy dropped her phone where she’d been playing some sort of game on it, judging by the dips and swoops she was making with it, and walked over. “You okay?”

“Super funny joke!” I said, forcing myself vertical again.

She looked at Astrid, mystified, who shrugged and said, “Apparently.”

“Okay, ‘cause I was about to win this duel.”

I glanced at her phone in alarm. “Duel?”

She showed me the screen where an avatar of some kind of ugly animated fairy swung a weapon. “It’s this game.”

Kristy chased her phone back to her booth.

“Well, uh, good luck,” I said.

I turned to Astrid. “The woo type?”

A faint smile curved her mouth. “It wasn’t that funny. Sample?” She held out a cup to a passing family.

Kristy’s phone emitted a jaunty melody and she said, “Dang it!”

I turned to her again as a guy across the tiny street near a honey stand whooped as his phone made the same noise.

I looked between them. “What happened?”

She glanced up. “I lost. And now I have to go to the Reflecting Pool.”

I was not the most on-topic conversationalist, but she had totally lost me. “Did you… Did that guy just duel you?”

She smiled. “Yeah, see, it’s one of those half-in-reality and half-online games. You can duel real people.”

I was vaguely horrified.

“Oh yeah!” Astrid piped up. “Everybody on campus is wandering around challenging each other. They’re actually trying to ban, what is it, Fae Kingdom? Some kid walked into a wall and broke his nose trying to beat an ogre.”

“Ouch.” I shuddered at the name and the nose. The fairies of myths were powerful, dangerous, capricious, and cruel. Witness their child stealing tactics.

Everything got a million times worse if you were tricked into fairyland. I wasn’t going to go messing around with fake fairylands.

“Of course,” Astrid continued. “Now the student union is up in arms about their first amendment rights, so I don’t think they’re going to get away with it.”

“The first amendment right to what—download an app?”

She shook her head. “Naw, there’s a message board in it, so I think they called it a publishing platform.”

“Clever.”

Kristy grinned. “All the campuses around here have a ton of quests and weapons stashes and taverns. So even if students couldn’t use it, I know a ton of people go to campuses anyway just for the goods.”

“So you have to go physical places in the city to pick up fake food at real pubs?”

She shrugged. “Well, you can just pay and get whatever you want wherever you want, but I’m cheap, so I actually have to go there. I took damage so I have to go to Slainge’s Well, which is the Reflecting Pool on the Mall. It’s cool. I’ve been to a bunch of places all over the city I didn’t know existed.”

I was surprised at the app’s mythological accuracy. Slainge’s Well was a place of real healing in Celtic mythology. “But the food is fake, right?”

She grinned. “That’s the cool part. You can pay with gold from the game to get real food at the pub. If they’ve signed up to be in the game. Or yeah, your character can just get life if you’re cheap and stand outside the place and get on their wifi.”

“We should try to get on the app to sell Mug Cakes!” Astrid said.

“I think the farmer’s market already is,” Kristy said and showed the screen with an old-fashioned map on it. “Yep! Definitely a refueling station, but you can set up as a vendor to take fake gold coins from the game.”

“I think we need to try to take credit cards before we take gold bullion,” Astrid said regretfully, and I nodded spastically, not sure why the app weirded me out.

“Sample?” Astrid asked in her customer service voice, and I turned back to the excruciating task of selling real food for real money.

12

It took forty-five minutes for me to stop feeling like I was hyperventilating and that the farmer market gods were going to come and kick me out for posing as a business. Not everybody who walked by stopped, and not everybody who took a sample, bought. But enough people did that I didn’t feel physically ill at all the free cakes we were giving out.

Astrid was counting cash in her backpack again.

“There won’t be magically more cash in there this time.”

Believe me, I’d tried it. I’d tried to bargain for more money, but nothing ever happened. Probably because money is just a symbol of labor or skills or time and energy of a human, and I’d have to bargain for all of it. I could never untangle all of what went into that particular dollar.

“If we do this good every week, we’ll break even and cover expenses in, well, hopefully by the end of the season.”

I waved to a person and offered a sample, but she said, “I’m gluten-free.”

Astrid looked hopefully at me, but I shook my head. “I’m not baking a gluten-free cake. That is not a thing.”

“The idea is that the people who bought it today liked it and come back and then we get a following so we get more money, like the Juyas of Dupont.” Astrid smiled and toasted me with her empty water bottle. “To being Juyas. This was really fun.”

I didn’t want to tell her it was by far the most horrible thing I’d done in a long time. I thought about the last few months: the dead bodies, fighting the second most powerful goblin in the world, stopping assassination attempts. I straightened in my chair. This wasn’t remotely close to the hardest thing I’d done, but why did it feel like that?

“Hey, how much fat is in this?” a dude asked in head-to-toe spandex.

“A lot. A lot of fat,” I said, and he sniffed and went away.

Another dude approached. He had a man bun, so I knew already how this was going to go. “Why are you even selling at a farmer’s market? It’s supposed to be for farmers.”

“So sorry,” I said mechanically.

When he stiffened, Astrid chimed in, “Thank you so much for your feedback. I come from a long line of farmers, and this is a traditional recipe.”

He sniffed too and moved on, doubtless to harass Kristy for selling metal.

“I hate this,” I said, and Astrid just laughed. “We’re not making diet cakes either,” I added.

“Oh hell no,” she said in her normal voice. “Jackass.”

I cackled. “So when you get super polite, I know to be on guard.”

She grinned. “Sometimes I forget where you’re from. Honey, there ain’t nobody on earth who can match a Southern girl when she decides to insult you.” Her accent went from typical DC with only the slightest twang to a deep Southern drawl.

“Weren’t you raised here? This isn’t that Southern.”

“Yes and no.” She pushed my shoulder. “DC is in the South, make no mistake. You’ve never heard the saying Northern manners and Southern efficiency? That’s DC.”

“Ouch.” I thought about it. “But really true.”

“My family is from Georgia, so I got the traditional education.”

“Kristy!”

We both pivoted to see an older version of Kristy with more freckles standing on the stoop of the Celtic Central shop.

Kristy jumped up. “Ma? What? I have another thirty minutes?”

“You know you can’t take Grandda’s knives to market!”

“I didn’t?”

The woman came out the door and walked down a few steps. “Bring them back inside!”

“I didn’t take them.” She stood up to show her mother the wires on the table.

“Oh, my god.” The woman sank down to the top step. “They’re gone too!”

I jumped up, toppling the chair and looking around. Kristy had come out of the shop a few hours ago, and the knives must have been there when she left, which meant I’d been right here as someone took them.

It was peak hours now and the market and street were crowded. I didn’t see anyone suspicious or running, but I did see a flash of blond hair.

The psychic. I just knew it.

“What’s going on?” Astrid asked.

I ignored her and jumped up on the microwave on the table with magic and then had to steady myself, shocked by the amount of power I had available. The bargain I made was living well for magic. As uncomfortable as the morning had been, apparently according to the inscrutable laws that govern bargains, selling cakes at the farmers market was an intense amount of living, which made for an intense amount of magic.

I turned in a circle, dancing on top of the microwave, looking for the psychic or miniature men and bowler hats, or maybe even somebody walking down the street with a bag of knives. Kristy was running to her mother and helping her up. They staggered toward the door of the shop. I kicked myself again; I was right here. Someone had taken them right under my nose.

I leaped off the table, at the last second remembering not to go invisible or leap straight to the front steps of the store behind Kristy. I landed nimbly but acceptably human near the table.

“Orange, what are you doing?”

I glanced back at Astrid who was gripping my turned-over chair and watching with round eyes.

“Um, just need to help Kristy. She’s a friend.”

I ran after the pair and managed to make it inside before they shut the door.

“You can’t come in here!” Kristy’s mom said as I dodged around her and headed for the case.

“It’s okay, Mom, she’s with the FBI.”

I paused. “Uh, right. Don’t touch anything, please.”

I reached the case. The glass was intact, and it was empty. I examined it quickly, chanting, “Clues, clues, clues.”

I had a tiny bit more knowledge about forensics from the cases I’d worked on, but not enough to help. The lock on the glass was intact. I was about to dash for the rear entrance when I stopped and turned back and squinted at the glass. I took a deep breath, leaned down, and let it out in a hot stream over the top of the glass. Nothing.

I sighed and then crouched down and did the same on the front panel of glass where the lock sat untouched. And there in the steam, the same symbol appeared. A circle made of blades around a knot. I dug for my phone and snapped a quick picture of both and then dialed “Agent Scharf” in my contacts. She’d entered her number into my phone when we first met, and yes, she put her first name as Agent.

As it was ringing, I turned back to the pair and said, “I'm serious, do not touch a thing. Stay with each other, go up to your home, and lock all the doors. Help is coming.” I didn’t think the thieves would take them too, but I didn’t want to take chances. The folks involved had a history of disappearing people.

“Scharf here,” my phone said.

“One sec,” I told my phone and said, “Where’s the back door?”

Kristy pointed.

“Thanks. Remember, stay together, and lock the door!”

I jumped over the counter, just brushing the cash register on my way over, not caring anymore if I looked human doing it. I glanced down as I sailed by and saw that some of the silver was missing too. So our thief didn't take everything in the shop; she was snooty about it.

“Orange!”

Startled, I glanced down at my phone. “Sorry! Somebody stole the rest of the knives from Celtic Central. They left another pattern on the glass. I’ve got to go.”

“Back up and explain,” Scharf shouted.

“I really can't. It was only seconds ago, and I'm going to try and find them. But we need the police to do their thing. Turns out I can't magic fingerprint dust.” I headed for a door out the back of the townhouse. It led to an alley in the middle of the block, flanked on both sides by the back of the townhouses. They were all old, maroon brick facades a few stories high that dominated the neighborhood.

“What are you talking about?”

I stopped for two seconds to roll my eyes and say, “I'd like to report a robbery at Celtic Central Irish shop. Send help.”

Then I hung up. My phone rang immediately, but I ignored it to look around. I saw movement at the end of the alley or thought I did. I spared a glance the other way mostly to make sure no one was watching. I used all of the juicy magic that came from selling cakes and bolted after the figure.

At the end of the block, I caught sight of someone running. They were shorter than I was. They didn’t have on a tricorn hat like the thieves in the video, but they were pale, short, and moving inhumanly quickly. It was a good bet. Maybe it wasn’t the psychic. Maybe she had accomplices.

I sped up. As a goblin, my strongest power was earth. And my body was made of a lot of earth. Which meant I could jump out of a building and keep all of my earth in place and undamaged. Running insanely quickly after a thief was nothing. I got to the end of the alley and burst out onto another neighborhood street of townhouses. I looked left and right and saw movement to my left away from the circle, deeper into the neighborhood.

I told everyone to forget me and started running again. I wasn't quite as fast because I was burning power to stay invisible and for speed, but I just hoped I was fast enough.

I rounded another corner and slammed into somebody. I rebounded off him and landed flat on my back and would have had a massive concussion if I hadn't been magicked to the gills. I sat up, dazed even with the magic, to see Cool perfectly collected and crouched on the ground a few feet away.

He patted his head and stood up slowly, not a hair out of place.

“Where are the knives?” I shouted as I jumped to my feet.

“I don’t have a clue what you’re talking about, muirnīn.”

“I’m not your darling.”

He blinked twice. Taken aback that I knew Gaelic? My father spoke to me in Scots Gaelic enough for me to stumble through the Irish version.

He dusted himself off and said, “No, of course, tá brón orm.”

I wanted to tell him he didn’t look sorry, but let it go. “You didn't see anybody really short go by here, or somebody with a really big bag? Or brandishing an ancient weapon?”

“Nothing here is ancient,” he said with no little irritation. “But no, nothing like that.”

“Then what are you doing here?”

“Going there.” He pointed. We were standing at the edge of a much smaller roundabout, only about forty feet across as opposed to a few hundred like Dupont. Taking up the skinny end of one block off the roundabout was a three-story building made of white stone with a green roof and fancy wrought iron window dressings with an Irish flag of green, white, and orange and a blue flag next to it. As the blue unfurled, I saw the stars and realized it was the European Union flag.

“That’s the Irish embassy. I am looking to get my Visa extended.”

“Oh.”

“I am sorry I ran into you.”

“Oh.”

“You’re not hurt?”

“No. Sorry too. I am. I mean I’m not hurt.” I shook my head. “I’m also sorry too.”

He crossed the roundabout, and I stared after him as he slipped inside. I cocked my head. He hadn’t been carrying anything, but fairies have glamours too. I spun in a circle, looking for the little thief who had at least been running and carrying something.

Nobody was around except a couple of kids in plaid private school uniforms.

I turned back the way I came. It took longer than I thought to get back to the farmer’s market. I was running faster than I thought. I felt beat up, body and soul.

There seemed to be a string of Embassies down this street now that I was going slow enough to notice. There was no rhyme or reason to the countries represented. The Embassy of Togo came next. I expected them to look like something from their countries, but aside from flags, they just looked like DC brownstones.

“Princess!”

I spun around, looked three feet lower, and spun around again. I saw a little blue goblin peeking from behind a bush. How did they find me?

“I need your help,” we said at the exact same time.

“Me first!” he said in a whining voice with a European accent I couldn’t quite place, something from the romance languages.

I crossed my arms. “Okay fine, what?”

“I beseech thee—”

“Nope, I don’t have time for this. How did you know where to find me?”

He cocked his head, “Princess?”

“How did you know where I was?”

He scratched through his blue hair. “The message board.”

“The what?”

I had been expecting a magical incantation. He fished out a battered phone and showed me an ancient website with a bunch of topics and comments. I copied the address into my phone and looked in dismay at the last few months of my life, all announced and commented on. At the bottom of the first page is said, “Peallaidh down.” The next topic was “He had a daughter.” The next one was “Track the Peallaidh.” I clicked on that one and saw a message from a goblin I didn’t know with an update “She’s at Dupont Circle.” I instinctively looked around, scanning the street, fighting horror. “This is how everybody knew where to go. This whole time, you all have just been updating a chat board from 1997?”

He bobbed his head.

“So somebody knows where I am all the time.”

This time the head bobbed side to side, and I couldn’t interpret what that meant.

“This whole time!”

“No. You move too quick, but you go the same places. Those someone watches.”

“This whole time! Here I thought I had magic GPS, and instead, I find Myspace’s earlier jankier cousin Goblin Talk.”

“What did you want?”

“Nothing!”

He rolly-pollied to get away from me and took off down the street. I turned in a circle. “Anybody else?”

A guy I was pretty sure was human made eye contact, looked horrified, and hurried away. In the roundabout, two people played the game dueling each other. I was beginning to recognize the distracted look of two people twitching near each other, staring at their phones. I resisted the urge to scream and started running.

The sirens and flashing police lights greeted me when I got back to the farmers market. They were setting up a perimeter around the front of the store. Kristy sat on the front steps. Scharf was speaking to them. I headed for her.

“Orange?” Astrid said from behind me.

I pivoted and winced. I’d forgotten all about her.

“Sorry! I’m so sorry.”

“What happened? What are all those cop cars doing here?”

I fidgeted with my hair, covering my ears, a useless gesture with my glamour on, but a very old nervous habit. “I’m sorry.”

“I always thought you disappear for days at a time because you're such a flake.”

I winced.

“I didn't mean it like that.”

“Yes, you did, and you’re mostly right. But sometimes I do this.”

“This being. . .”

“Orange!” Scharf shouted.

I groaned. I’d never had so many people shouting my name before.

“And the cops know your name.”

“She’s with the FBI.”

Astrid literally took a step back.

“Astrid, please.”

“No, you have to go!” She spun. The table was already empty. She was dodging through the crowd before I could stop her, surprisingly nimble with the big backpack on her back.

I turned back to Scharf with a sigh. “I think we have a mole in our special task force.”

She raised a brow.

“I talk about these knives, and one day later they’re gone?” I was going to mention the psychic or Cool, but I didn't have any proof, and the little man I’d chased seemed to blow up that theory anyway. “I chased someone away.”

“Where?” she asked.

“Kristy’s mom came outside and said the knives were gone.”

“When?”

“Like, ten minutes ago. Seconds before I called you.” When she looked down at her phone, I took the opportunity to roll my eyes. I’d sat in on a lot of witness questioning; it was much less fun when it was directed at me. “So I went inside the shop, looked at the case.”

“Did you touch it?” she asked, digging out the little notebook from her front pocket.

“Pretend I’m the slightest bit competent,” I said with a bite to my voice I couldn’t suppress.

“Sorry.” Her hands dropped. “We didn’t find your fingerprints anyway. I don’t know why I asked. What happened next?” She pulled her notebook back up. I wanted to rip it out of her hands.

“I breathed on the case.”

The notebook went back down. “What?”

“To see if I could see a pattern.”

“Oh right, sorry.”

She put pen to notebook, and I grabbed it. “Stop that.”

“Orange.”

“Please, let’s just talk for a second like normal people.”

She raised one eyebrow and smiled. “In the middle of a crime scene.”

“Scharf.”

“Okay, okay. What happened next?”

I waited until she put the notebook away. “I ducked out the back of the shop, and I saw someone running at the end of the block. He was short, wearing black, moving fast.”

“Magically fast?”

“What did I tell you about jokes?”

“That wasn’t a joke.” She ran a hand through her hair and resettled her sunglasses amongst the locks. “One of the dudes from the video?”

“I couldn’t tell. When I rounded a corner, he was gone.”

“Shit.”

“Sorry.”

“Not your fault.”

“I was going to say we could pull security camera footage. But I doubt Celtic Central has one.”

“Definitely no,” I said with feeling, but not for the same reason. I couldn’t remember if I had gone invisible or not before turboing through the streets of DC.

Scharf turned away toward the street.

“Wait, aren’t we going in?”

“Do you think there’s anything more to learn from an empty glass case?”

“Honestly, no, but that’s never stopped you before,” I said with a shrug.

She laughed. “Crime Scene has it. We have another lead to follow.”

“You found the dirt!”

“No, struck out. Maria is doing more research on possible sites.”

“What then?”

She smiled. “Art crimes traced a sale back to the security guard.”

“O’Connor didn’t kidnap his own kid!”

“No, the one who replaced him. The one we couldn't find. Apparently, he was sitting on a ton of the art.”

“Crap. I mean, that’s good.”

“Let’s go see what they found.”

I sighed and looked back at the empty table where are fledgling business was launched. “Yeah, I have a feeling I suddenly have some time on my hands.”

13

I don’t know what I expected the house of an art thief to look like, but this wasn't it. We were in an old neighborhood in the southeast part of the city. It was more spread out than the ubiquitous townhouses closer to the center, but strangely the houses weren’t any bigger than in town. Tiny bungalows sat on large lawns enclosed by chain-link fences. We pulled up to one that was half brick and half yellow siding, both looking a little worse for wear. Their huge lawn was mostly dead.

It looked like a worn version of every other house on the street. The only distinguishing characteristic was the law-enforcement vehicles parked up and down the street, and a veritable chain of agents and crime scene techs stretching from those vehicles into the backyard. A few of them stood at the front door talking to an older woman who was holding back two squirrelly gray dogs, but most of them were heading around the house.

Scharf got out of the car in a hurry and said, "I hope we’re not too late."

She slammed the door and I paused for a moment. "Too late for what?"

When she tapped the front of her hood on her way around the car, I scrambled out after her. I had to resist telling the world not to notice me. An orange line led directly behind me to Scharf’s car. Weirdly, seeing the escape route gave me the courage to follow her. I tried to imagine grabbing her keys and driving away, but that seemed too cowardly even for a goblin.

Scharf also ignored the house and followed the agents into the backyard. It was just as dead as the front yard but there were a couple of nice little trees by the back chain-link fence.

A middle section of the fence had sagged almost to the ground, and one agent was playing chicken with a bulldog in the neighbor’s yard as he screamed at said neighbor, an old lady in a purple polyester housecoat, to get her dog under control. All the barking was a little overwhelming with the little corgi between her legs and the two dogs still stuck in the house.

The center of the action was right in the middle of the yard where techs swirled around a backyard shed in robin’s egg blue twenty years younger than the house. Some techs were sorting through gardening supplies and sports equipment on blue tarps in front of the shed.

Scharf stopped just inside the fence and scanned quickly. Not for the first time, I wondered what she saw when she looked at this chaos. I knew it was a choreographed dance of techs and agencies, but it was one I barely understood.

What I noticed beyond the emptying shed was that about every third tech walked out slower than the others holding something carefully in their hands. They did not dump it in the grass with the other detritus but walked it carefully out of the yard.

I walked closer, keeping one eye out for dog poop, and saw one tech walk out with a glass tumbler. There was a decorative design around the lip, but it didn't look obviously Celtic. It did look old. I forgot about dog poop.

I felt a strange mix of elation, disappointment, and frustration when I saw the art. Mostly on behalf of Scharf. She'd been certain she would be the one to solve it with me since the disappearing thieves were magical. But apparently, the mastermind lived in a bungalow in Southeast DC.

I frowned. There was no way a museum exhibit’s worth of treasure could fit in that shed.

I glanced back at Scharf and saw her speaking to one of the techs. I moseyed closer to the shed and tried to see if it was the start of a basement complex for stolen goods. It was hard to see in the dim light, but all the techs in the shed were on one level. They stood out in their bright white clean suits. If there was a lower level, the entrance was tiny.

"Orange!"

I spun around and saw Scharf beckoning me with her tilted head. I jogged over, and she pointed to the side of the house where a familiar agent stood in the shade with a crowd of people. Axel Alexander, the Viking who assisted at the Hoover building, stood confronting a bunch of other agents.

I didn't recognize any of them but knew they were agents from their posture, uniform, and weapons. Scharf said that there was no real FBI requirement for a buzz cut or khaki, but they were popular choices. The only non-agent of the group was a kid at the center. He was skinny, a foot shorter than all of them, and wearing jeans and a ripped black T-shirt.

Scharf bobbed her head again without saying anything, and we walked closer.

“Who was your fence?” Axel asked the kid whose eyes ping-ponged between various agents.

“Didn’t!” he squealed.

“This is our investigation, Agent,” one of the others said to Axel, making his job title sound like an insult, which was strange considering he probably had the same title.

“Where is the rest of it?" Axel asked, undeterred.

“Man!”

“And this is our arrest!" the man shouted.

“Arrest!" The kid set an octave higher, which I had thought impossible.

I jumped when Scharf put a hand on my shoulder. “I'm going to try and separate Axel and art crimes from the kid. You have to make a bargain. Ask him about the thieves.”

I gaped that her. "You're not going to arrest him?"

"I won't have to.” She paused and admitted, “I can’t. It’s not my case. Art crimes will take him, and they are going to interrogate him for days. But he is not on that tape and there are dozens more who are, and he is our best link to them."

I nodded. "And art crimes doesn't know they exist."

She glanced at the sky as if the wispy clouds in the anemic blue would send help. “The task force has a few wrinkles to work out. I liked it better when we could go wherever we wanted, and nobody would know you were there.”

My hands clasped at my breastbone of their own accord. "We can go back to that! I would be okay with that!"

“You can't put the cat back in the bag."

“That's not how that cliché goes. That's not what it means… Never mind.”

“You game?”

I looked at the pile of agents, feeling even more naked. A bunch more orange lines belted to various corners of the yard with exit ramps.

“Orange?"

"Yeah. Yeah.”

She started toward the agents. One spun the kid, clicked handcuffs on his wrists, and started hauling him toward the front yard.

He glanced back at Axel and said, “Guess it wasn’t very unexplained.”

I heard Scharf curse and echoed her. Axel didn’t react at all.

I told everyone not to notice me and ran for the agent and the kid. Fortunately, the whole crowd of them didn't feel the need to escort one skinny young man to his doom. Unfortunately, once we got to the front yard, we had an even bigger audience of looky-loos—neighbors and press—gathered around the vehicles. When the agent emerged with the kid, there was a flurry of movement and flashing cameras.

I cursed and told the world not to notice me, except for the kid. I had thirty feet to make a bargain. "Deal, tell me you didn’t do it and I’ll… I’ll…” My brain froze. In a bargain for the truth, I also had to give them something. Something they genuinely wanted. What did a young man who is about to go to jail want, besides a lawyer which I didn’t exactly have in my back pocket?

The kid blinked at me.

“I didn’t do it!” he shouted, and I groaned. This kept happening. Without a bargain, his confession was useless.

“Tell it to a judge,” the agent escorting him said. We all paused as the kid’s phone started beeping in his back pocket. It was a familiar waterfall of notes I recognized from the game Kristy had been playing at the Farmer’s Market.

“Harry?” a kid shouted from the crowd. He was skinny, blond, and about the same age as the suspect.

“I’m going to miss our quest!” Harry shouted.

“You okay?” the blond asked.

“What quest?” the agent asked. “Is that your fence?”

“What? You guys saw my fence! Is that really what all this is about? I was gonna repair it! Mrs. Chesterfield calls the police when a squirrel gets into her bird feeder! If she said a sagging fence is a crime…”

“That ain’t the crime, kid,” the agent said, looking a little sympathetic for the first time in their exchange.

“I didn’t do it!”

I jumped up to him, seizing my last chance as we approached the car. “I’ll… I’ll pat you on the back if you repeat that. Not just repeat that.” It wouldn’t help me if I bargained for him to say the words. “If you tell me the truth about your involvement, deal!” I said desperately.

“I didn’t have anything to do with it!”

Nimbly, I got a hand on his back for a pat as the agent maneuvered him into the black sedan parked right out front and leaped out of the way as the door shut. I waited for the chime. Nothing happened.

“Wait!” I shouted.

The agent slammed the driver’s door and took off. Obviously, he wasn’t going to wait for the goblin he couldn’t see. There was no chime. What did that mean?

Scharf jogged up. “Did you get it?”

I slumped. “He did it.”

“What? What about the little dudes on the tape?”

I nodded and then shook my head. “No, obviously, they did it. But he…” I played back his words.

“What?”

“He had something to do with it. Not that he did it.”

“What bargain did you make?”

“He said he didn’t have anything to do with it.” I left out the part about patting him on the back. “But there was no chime, so the bargain isn’t fulfilled. So he did have something to do with it. That doesn’t necessarily mean he did it. I mean, obviously, we watched it happen, and he wasn’t there. So he didn’t literally do it.”

Scharf gripped her hair. “How are we going to talk to him now?”

“I’m sorry. I’d really love to, you know, solve that one, but I don’t think that’s my half of this bargain we’ve got going between us.”

She let go of her hair. “I’ll talk to Axel.”

I heard a chime, and for a second, I thought I had some strange new magic. It repeated, and I realized it was a real sound anyone could hear. I looked at the blond kid now typing furiously on his phone.

“You go talk to Axel,” I said absently and made for the kid.

He didn’t react when I stepped in front of him. I sighed and backed up, stooped down like I was tying a shoe, and let go of my unnoticing. I stood up and pivoted so I was facing the crime scene.

“This is crazy!” I said to him, digging out my phone to snap a picture like everyone else was doing. “Isn’t this crazy?”

“They just arrested Harry!”

“Wait,” I said, filling my voice with wonder. “Did you know him?”

“Yeah. We were going to play today.”

“Play?”

He looked down at his phone where the chime sounded again. “I guess we just forfeited.”

“The app game thing?”

“Fae Kingdom. We were at the top of the leaderboard!”

“How sad for you,” I said, and he glanced at me. I winced. I just couldn’t find any sympathy. “I heard he stole a bunch of art from that museum where he worked! Crazy!”

He gaped. “Is that what this is about? Man! We didn’t do it!”

I looked at the sky. I had to get a bunch of helpful people who just volunteered confessions without the slightest prompting. Of course, to most, a denial isn’t a confession, but with a bargain, I didn’t have that problem. “I’ll make you a deal, I will listen to you talk about the fairy kingdom if you also tell me about the crime.”

“What are you, a journalist?”

I scoffed. “Do I look like a journalist?”

“I don’t know anything about the crime! Why should I even tell you?”

“You’re right. There’s absolutely no reason. I don’t know what I was thinking. Why don’t you tell me about fairies?” And I could get this bargain over with. I would have just walked away, but unfulfilled bargains itched for a while.

“Just download it. It’s free. It’s really cool. You can play quests all over the city and get really cool things. This is one of the best places to play since there’s so much historical shit.”

“Ah yes, the famed DC historical shit. Must-see.”

He laughed at that. “Harry was the best, 'cause he could get us into all these places with his job.” We both froze and belatedly a lazy chime sounded in my head.

“I have an alibi!” the kid wailed. “We weren’t even in DC. My sister dragged me to New York to watch the musical.”

“Ah yes, the famed New York musical.” I sagged. I could have bargained to ensure he was telling the truth, but if he wasn’t even in the city, that would be easy to check. I was about to walk away when a new thought occurred to me. Maybe he wasn’t the only person Harry had played after hours in a museum? Or who had played Harry?

I blinked wide round eyes, knowing I looked guileless at less than five feet tall. “That’s super cool. Where’d you guys go?”

“Not to any museum! Definitely not after hours or anything! I gotta go.” Apparently guileless was going around.

As soon as he disappeared into the crowd, I pivoted. “Scharf!”

She was deep in conversation with Axel. I clenched my teeth and dug my phone out of my back pocket.

My thumb shook as I hit download Fae Kingdom.

Once it loaded, it opened it to a fanfare of wailing bagpipes and a jumping fish. The screen welcomed me to Tír na nÓg, the Irish fairy world. It let me pick my avatar first. I was surprised to see historically accurate folklore figures like the Morrigan, Lugh, Dagda, and Brigit, not the usual half-dressed girls with wings.

The welcome screen looked innocuous with some generic Celtic scrolling and a prompt to sign in. I did so with a totally made-up email address and chose my character, a little orange goblin I named Blue77, because I have no sense of self-preservation. The spinning wheel gave way to a map in an explosion of sparks and flowers. Someone had poured a lot of money into this.

The map looked like old paper with ink-drawn lines, but in a moment, I realized they had laid it over a real map. After I accepted it’s request to know my location, I zoomed in and saw I was standing on a street in the game that matched my real location. A prompt asked if I wanted to go visible to the players around me, and I hit no so fast I had to go back and check my settings, terrified I hit the wrong button.

I zoomed out and saw little icons all over DC, Maryland, and Virginia. I clicked on a few of them. They seemed to be a combination of private businesses where you could get food for your avatar and historical sites where you could get various things vaguely related to what really happened at that monument. I snickered. In the heart of DC, the monument to Civil War Battle Field Nuns that so upset Carlisle was a place to get a bandage.

I swiped sideways until I hit the National Mall where I saw they’d turned the Reflecting Pool into Slainge’s Well like Kristy had talked about. I found the American History Museum and zoomed in.

A little popup told me that I could resupply my weapons here, participate in a quest against the Vikings, grab a bite to eat, or duel others in the area if I was in the museum. Or I could do it from my current location if I paid the low monthly fee of $9.99 for the deluxe version.

Out of curiosity, I hit the main menu to get out of the map and see what else I could do. I found a number of quests I could undertake, teams I could join, and message boards to coordinate duels. The gameplay seemed to be a combination of wandering around the city getting stuff, dueling other players near you, and more elaborate quests in certain locations. It looked like fun.

If I was going to play, I needed a weapon. I saw I could either go somewhere like the museum to pick one up or do an anytime quest that would net me rewards.

I started with the easiest quest, which asked me whether I believed in fairies. I hit yes. I fervently believed in fairies. A familiar trill of notes sounded, and in the right-hand corner, I earned two points of eternal life. It was a strange name for your health stats, given you were constantly losing them.

“You’re invisible.”

I looked up, heart pounding, wondering what magical creature had caught me. I had to adjust my gaze down again at a girl a foot shorter than I was waving a phone that cost three times what mine did. She was wearing a grubby t-shirt and jeans with her white-blond hair in a messy ponytail. She looked human, and I remembered I’d let go of my unnoticing to talk to Harry’s friend.

“Since you can see me, I’m clearly not invisible?”

“You’re playing Fae? We can duel, but you’re invisible. On the map.”

“I don’t want to duel. I just started.”

Her eyes lit up. “I’m the best. I even beat Harry!”

I crouched down. “The dude who lived there?”

She nodded. “He was the best. But now I’m the best, ‘cause I beat him. He says he’s gonna play a tournament this weekend that will blow me into the water.”

“I think you mean out of the water.”

“Nuh unh. You got to go to the well if you get dead. Into the water! My mom won’t pay, so I got to pretend I care about h’story to go to the Mall and get undead.”

There were so many horrifying implications to that sentence, I didn’t know where to start, but I was fairly sure she was speaking metaphorically. “When you lose your points in the game, you have to refill them by going to the National Mall and the Reflecting Pool?”

She nodded then shook her head. “Or you can buy more life, but like I said…”

“Real money. I get it.” I looked at her avatar on her screen, a stereotypical fairy with sparkly wings and a wicked-looking, historically accurate spiked mace. I was glad we hadn’t dueled.

“It’s okay, ‘cause when we’re on the Mall, I can get in some serious quests that are worth so much.”

My heart rate spiked. What had the other boy said? That Harry got him into a bunch of places no one else can. “Where you duel matters?”

“Nuh unh.”

There went that theory.

“Where you quest matters. But most quests have dueling, so I guess?”

“Did Harry ever come with you to the Mall?”

She nodded once, punching things into her phone.

“Did Harry ever bring you with him to his work?”

She grinned and nodded again. “It’s a s’cret, so I can’t say why or when or wherever to anyone. But that’s how I beat him.”

“Good job keeping that secret!”

“Marie? Get over here! What are you doing?”

The kid, Marie, jumped and ran toward an older, taller version of her without another word, tucking the huge phone into the waistband of her jeans at the small of her back.

Harry was involved. He was the best at Fae Kingdom, a game that let you duel people in random locations around the city. What would happen if you dueled someone after hours in a museum no one else could get to? How many points would that be worth?

I clicked on the top name in the rankings to see their profile. There was no hint of identity, just a long list of quests completed and a list of vanquished foes. My heart picked up. Was I looking at Harry? I clicked on it, and a list of ninety usernames unfurled. The foes he had vanquished.

If I was right and Harry had let someone into the museum that night, they were on this page.

“Scharf!”

I scrolled through them, rolling my eyes when I saw Cú Chulainn, the famed Celtic Warrior, the President, and a few famous baseball players on the list. I stopped on one ephemeral fairy who called herself “Message me for psychic readings.”

“Scharf!”

14

“I’m telling you, the psychic plays the game. I saw her playing it at the farmer’s market,” I told Scharf as we rode an elevator toward the top floors of the Hoover Building.

“Fantastic theory. But we can’t arrest someone because we saw a username that may or may not belong to her in a list of vanquished foes of a username that may or may not belong to a kid with a bunch of stolen art in his shed that he didn’t steal.”

“But why can’t we go talk to her?” I asked as the doors opened on a fancy hallway.

“You mean, instead of interrupting an interrogation in the Hoover Building.”

She took an abrupt right and left me scrambling after her. “I mean instead of doing anything in the Hoover Building,” I muttered.

“I heard that.”

Abruptly she slowed to a crawl, and I had to use a pulse of magic to keep from running into her.

“You bargain,” she said. “We get his username. We get his movements that night. We get him to admit he let a woman in. We get him to identify a photo of her. And then we go talk to the psychic.”

“That simple, hunh?” I asked as she knocked on one door in the hallway.

I checked the nameplate. “Giudice!”

“And somehow we do it all without alerting art crimes to the fact that their thief is a fairy and the investigating officer is a goblin,” she said.

“I heard that,” I echoed.

“And somehow make sure our interrogation doesn’t end up on camera that everybody and the jury will see.”

“I’m beginning to hate this place,” I said.

“Everybody hates this place,” Scharf replied. “The building is falling apart but nobody will fix it, and the idiots from Washington hand down decrees like they have any idea of what it's like in the field today.”

I squinted at her. “Aren’t you technically an idiot from Washington?”

She shook her head. “I’m at a field office, like any other field office in the country.”

“Which is in Washington.”

“Totally different.”

Axel opened the door and ushered us into a tiny office. “What do you need?”

“To talk to him.”

“He’s very busy.”

“He’s going to want—“

“For me to not distract him with meaningless problems,” Axel finished for her.

“I know how the thieves entered the museum.”

Axel crossed his arms. “Presumably they used a door.”

I snickered and both of them looked at me. I shrank down into my collar. “That was funny.”

“You’re telling me you know which door?” Axel demanded.

Scharf shifted her weight. “No.”

“You’re telling me that you know better than the entire cyber crimes division of Metro PD and art crimes how people got in. Even though they’ve scoured every inch of every videotape of the entire week before the crime.”

“No.”

He gestured toward the door.

“I have a list of ninety names. The thief is one of them.”

Axel held out a hand.

“Giudice,” Scharf said, enunciating each syllable.

“What do you need?” a voice asked from behind us.

We all pivoted to see the big man himself standing in the hallway.

I opened my mouth and Scharf shook her head infinitesimally. I shut my mouth.

Scharf cleared her throat. “I need to interrupt an interview.”

“Is this the museum theft? Art crimes has a suspect in custody.”

“That’s the interview I need to interrupt.”

“Why? We caught him. They’ll get his accomplices out of him and… Well, and that’s it. Not that I wish NTEPUEL couldn’t have had our day.”

I opened my mouth, and Scharf stepped on my foot. I closed my mouth.

“He’s being framed for the theft. The few pieces we found in his shed were a plant. And we have the name of his accomplice.”

Giudice walked in and closed the hallway door.

“Who?” he said with a light in his eye I didn’t like.

Scharf looked at me.

“Oh, so now I can talk?”

“Orange,” Scharf said in a pleasant voice that still managed to convey how much she would like to strangle me.

I explained the app and the vanquished foes list.

“They’re not on the video!” Axel said when I mentioned that Harry was inviting people into the museum after hours for online duels.

I pivoted. “Are they not on the video, or did you just not notice them?”

“We need to talk to that boy,” Giudice said slowly.

Scharf all but sagged. “Yes, sir. That is why I am here.”

He pivoted and wrenched open the door and strode out. This time Scharf scrambled to keep up. Axel and I looked at each other and ran after them.

“Are… Are you doing the interview, sir?” Scharf asked when we’d wound to a stop by the elevator.

He raised one eyebrow. “I’m curious what diplomatic way you’re going to suggest I don’t.” The corner of his mouth quirked up.

I snickered again. He played the jolly old uncle, but this dude was sharp.

Scharf grinned. “I’m sure you would do so much better than any of us that it almost wouldn’t be fair. I really would like the practice, sir.”

The elevator dinged and we trooped on.

“That was excellent. Top-notch groveling. Axel, take note.”

Axel did not take note. He looked ready to murder Scharf. She looked amused. I realized she liked Giudice.

“Don’t worry,” Giudice said, hooking a thumb at me. “She’s going to do the interview.”

“Me!”

“Oh yes, I’ve heard so much about your interviews.”

“I… We… What?” I felt cold all over and then hot.

“It’s really a team effort,” Scharf said, all trace of humor gone.

Giudice waved a hand. “Whatever your process. I want to see this.”

Orange lines spouted through a ceiling panel in the elevator as well as the main doors. If my magic thought I could get out of a tiny metal box plunging toward the ground, it was really desperate.

I glanced at Scharf, shaking my head like I could stop this. She nodded. We had an entire conversation like two bobbleheads shaking and nodding until the elevator doors opened. I had no idea who won.

We wound down a set of short stairs to a small collection of desks opposite a series of closed doors. A number of agents gathered around a little white speaker on one closed door that was spouting people's voices. A few more stood by a computer across from the door watching something I couldn’t see on the screen.

“What are they doing?”

“Watching the interview. He’s in there,” she said.

“What about the fancy double mirrors and recording equipment and all of that?”

Scharf chuckled. “We only started recording interviews a few years ago. They're retrofitting, but not to the point of fancy mirrors. And I think that project is taking a backseat to keeping this place standing. There's a camera inside and recording equipment, and it's getting streamed out here.”

“On a computer from the nineties?” It had an old-school monitor that stuck out behind the screen for another foot.

We marched up. Scharf shook hands with a few people watching and traded branches and service years in shorthand only they understood.

“We’re going to take over for a few minutes,” Giudice said heartily. I was beginning to fear his Santa act.

The camaraderie noticeably died. Until he’d said that, the art crimes dudes had seemed thrilled to be talking to him.

“Sir?” one of them finally got the guts to say.

“My team, can we interrupt for a sec?”

“You want to put in a completely new team for an interrogation of the biggest art thief in the history of the Smithsonian?” another asked like we were asking to declare the end of Art Crimes.

“Yes,” Giudice said. “Scharf, take it away.”

Then he turned to the monitor where I could just see Harry sitting at a table in a grainy feed on the screen. Giudice took one of the chairs abandoned by the team when we’d arrived and focused on the screen.

All of us stared at his back.

Scharf recovered first. “Please pull your men out.”

One man, the oldest of the group, although I don't know if that translated into rank, pulled us away from Giudice, “So what the hell is this new task force?”

Another chimed in, “And what is it doing in our investigation?”

Scharf just smiled. “Should I just burst in there or…”

“You're going to break the rhythm of my guy. And the rapport. It's going to set us back half a day.”

Scharf just shrugged. I gulped, realizing this wasn't going to be me invisible in the corner feeding Scharf questions and her taking the hit if it didn't work. My name was on this.

I’d made dozens of bargains and got to the truth every time. Almost every time. But no one had ever watched me do it.

She made a move toward the door, and I gulped. I still didn’t have anything for my half of the bargain. The only thing I thought he could want was a lawyer, and I’d be a dead goblin if I offered that.

“I’ll explain everything later,” Axel said in the syrupy voice he only used around his boss. “But let’s just humor them for now, okay? You can come in and save the kid from them in ten minutes and look like the damn hero to him.”

The dude looked a little bit happier. “Okay, fine.”

He opened the door.

Desperately, I looked around and saw a root beer next to the ancient computer monitor. I picked it up. The one guy who did not look like an FBI agent sitting at the computer in a soggy white button-down over arms that had rarely ever done a pushup said, “That’s mine!”

I stiffened my spine. “I am confiscating this. For the interrogation.”

He sputtered but I glanced at Scharf and said, “Bargain.”

“We definitely need that,” she said.

A few walked away muttering as Scharf held open the door for me. I looked around my first official FBI interrogation room. Despite all my brushes with law enforcement, I’d never been in one before.
