“You missed me fighting a god and losing and barely escaping. And when I figured out what he was trying to do to you, you missed me protecting you by keeping you out of it and calling on the people—goblins—No, people who could actually do something about it. Little games, my aching goblin butt.”

Scharf shoved her hands in the pockets of her jeans and pivoted to stare at the festival looking lost.

From back here, the games looked like small, innocent fun. A simple gathering of people wearing green. The bagpipe had given way to a guitar player I couldn’t really hear. I could see a few of the goblins still working the crowd, including Dun and Mrs. Henderson, but no leprechauns. Were they conceding or falling back for reinforcements? I couldn’t see Cool. I took a breath, ready to keep explaining.

“Okay,” Scharf said, staring at the toes of her boots; one of which she scuffed into the dirt in a very un-Scharf-like gesture.

“Okay?” I hunched and put my back against the monument next to her.

“Yeah, okay. If that’s what happened.” She scrubbed her face. “A puppet? My god, if that’s what happened, I was a liability not an asset. You were right to keep me out of it.”

“Okay?” I couldn’t believe it. Scharf could not be conceding.

She gave a little shrug. “I don’t love it, but there’s a reason for need-to-know. There’s a reason you have a chain of command where everyone is only given the essentials, for just this situation.” She straightened up, looking more like herself. “Well, maybe not this exact situation.”

I squinted at her, still not trusting it. She looked totally calm. I suddenly felt like I had been a little naive, believing her absolute truth-telling. She was a straight shooter, yes, but she was a straight shooter in a bureaucracy of national secrets and deadly missions even before I came on the scene. I wondered for a second if I knew her at all.

“Just out of curiosity…” she said slowly.

“Cool.”

“The Irish guy?”

“The Irish demi-god with a weapon that can kill someone in one blow.”

“And I just did whatever he said.”

“You’re really calm about this.”

She bit her lip, staring into the middle distance. “It really explains some things. I kind of remember it. Didn’t like it. How’d he do it?”

“He learned your names. And stole your will. They do that. Fairies”

She barked out a laugh. “Fairies again.”

“Don’t underestimate him,” I said, tugging on her sleeve. “He’s not a will-o-the-wisp. He’s one of the most famous warriors in human history. And non-human…”

She looked at the group, hands still in her pockets. “And he’s here, with the art.”

“I haven’t seen him or the art,” I felt impelled to confess.

She cocked an eyebrow.

“But yeah.”

“Okay,” she said again. “Sorry, I jumped down your throat.”

She started toward her car.

“I… Scharf!”

She pivoted.

“You’re just going to leave?”

“I was.” She looked a little confused. “Shouldn’t I? If he can do what you say, it sounds like I shouldn’t be here.”

“Are you okay?”

She opened her mouth and closed it. “I don’t know.”

“Maybe you should go.”

“That’s the reason you lied, isn’t it? I wasn’t okay? You left the wounded back at camp and went out into the fray.”

“There was no camp,” I countered feeling a little lost.

“That was a metaphor.”

“Oh, yeah. Then I did.” I gave a half-shrug, unaccountably ashamed. “Yeah.”

She gave a humorless laugh. “Our bargain is over.”

“What? No.”

“You help me with cases, and I don’t arrest you. But nobody is going to arrest you. The president himself pardoned you. And you haven’t been helping me for a long time.”

“Yes, I have!” I said with no little professional outrage. “I’ve solved two murders for you.”

“That’s what I mean. I’ve been helping you.”

I had never seen it like that, but the second, the very millisecond I did, a huge, clanging chime sounded in my head. The end of a bargain, the biggest one I’d ever made. I fell to my knees and said, “Ow.”

She dove for me, “What’s wrong?”

She put a hand under my shoulder to try to get me upright, and I batted her away. I shook my head which was still ringing. “Nothing, I’m fine!”

I climbed to my feet as the gong faded away. My heart beat like I’d just run a marathon.

“What now?” I asked, “I still have to…” I gestured toward the games. “Scharf?”

She met my eyes. “Now we trust each other. Isn’t that what the little goblin at the battlefield said? A bargain is to prove your trust. You’ve proved it.”

“Even though I literally wiped your memory, lied to you, and cut you out of your own investigation?”

“You’ve clearly never joined the army. And it shows.”

I frowned. “You still can’t tell a joke. That’s just not allowed.”

She laughed. It was weak, but it happened.

“Okay then,” I said when she didn’t say anything else. This was better. Objectively better. But why did I suddenly feel worse? More nervous? Maybe I was more goblin than I thought, relying on the magical compulsion that comes with a bargain. I felt untethered.

A dog began to bark and then a second. I didn’t really consciously hear it; Scharf was the first to turn.

When I followed her gaze, I didn’t see anything. Guitar guy was still at it. Astrid was still at the booth. Except…

There was sand flying out of the volleyball court that wasn’t exactly obeying the laws of physics. I couldn’t see what was throwing it. Then I heard somebody yell something in Gaelic I didn’t understand, and I remembered the barking dogs that seem to follow Cool wherever he went. I went hot all over.

Then I heard the high, keening cry of a small creature in pain.

“No!” I ran for them without a second thought. “No, no, no!”

I bolted under the sign for the games and didn’t bother trying to avoid either the market or the stage but just vaulted over both on my way to the courts, telling everybody to forget me.

Cool stood on the far side of the net in his old regalia that matched his avatar in the app, a padded shirt strapped to his chest with leather thongs and leather pants around his legs. Something lay at his feet, something that blended very well with the sand.

“No!” I skidded through the sand, under the net, and saw Dun crumpled at Cool’s feet, his eyes staring sightlessly and his neck at an odd angle. I crawled near to him and cradled his head in my lap. It flopped with a crunch. He was gone, his brown skin graying already. I wondered what everybody else saw.

“Banphrionsa,” Cool said with satisfaction.

I looked up at him with a strange feeling in my gut—grief, rage, and fear all mixed together. I didn’t have a word for it. “I am not a princess.”

He stooped down, his dark hair falling over the leather strap around his forehead and spit. I caught it with my insignificant water magic that only worked a few feet from me. I never thought it would come in handy. I thrust it back in his face. If I hoped to disconcert him, I failed. He wiped it off calmly and said, “Gataige.”

“I don’t know what that means,” I said.

He got right down next to my ear and said, “Thief.”

“You’re the thief,” I said and tried to pick up Dun, but his limps flopped. “And a murderer!”

When the crowd gasped, I realized we were attracting attention. I heard mutterings. “Is he really dead?”

“Someone should call the police.”

Scharf stepped forward with her badge. She was off duty, but of course, she had her badge. “FBI. Everybody step back.”

Cool turned with a strange light in his eyes.

“Plug your ears!” I screamed.

He shouted, “Laura Scharf, come to me.”

Scharf started singing at the top of her lungs, “Oh say can you see…” She had a passable alto that wasn’t too flat. People noticed and started hastily standing up and putting their hands over their hearts, doffing caps. Cool opened his mouth again, and I used magic to enhance my grip on the front of his tunic to yank him away.

“I challenge thee,” I said. He grinned and donned a leather glove and fished a very modern smartphone out of a slit in his leather jerkin. I knocked it out of his hand. “Not in Tír na nÓg. Right here, Setanta.” I said, using the name he got when he was born.

He flexed and pushed me off him. He was strong, but the power from all the quests was flowing, and he wasn’t stronger than me.

I gulped. This wasn’t going to be a battle of wits like my last duel with a goblin where we both walked away smiling. I put Dun’s lifeless body gingerly into the sand.

I didn’t want to best Cool. I wanted him dead.

24

Cool pulled a spear out of the ether, and I clenched a fist. Fist versus spear. This was going to be a short fight. I glanced at Dun. I had asked him here. I had done this to him, and I couldn’t walk away.

Cool spun the spear. I couldn’t just fight him with fists either. “Where is it?”

“Where is what?” he asked, spinning the knife again.

“The treasure. The things you stole. I want one of the knives. They’re on my continent, and they belong to me.”

He laughed and pointed toward the Potomac.

“You put it in the river?” I vaguely recalled that the entrance to the other world was sometimes underwater. “You put it in fairyland.”

“Go and get it. Open a door.”

I crossed my arms; I was still brimming with the power from the quests, and something told me that door wouldn’t close when I was done. “Nice try.”

He rolled his eyes, stuck his hand out, and it disappeared.

He pulled a wicked knife with a round handle and a beautiful blade out of nowhere.

It matched the ones I’d seen in Celtic Central. The treasure was already in Tír na nÓg. What did that mean for us? For our chance at getting it back? One problem at a time, Orange.

I took it and hefted it, nicking a knuckle on the blade. I had precisely zero weapons training.

Scharf finished up the national anthem with the fastest final line in history and ran for me, her hands still over her ears.

“You can’t stop me!” I shouted.

“Give me the knife!” Scharf shouted back, her fingers still plugged in her ears.

“Laura Scharf!” Cool said, looking like he’d just received a beautiful present.

“Give me the knife, Orange.”

I spun to confront Cool. “You can’t have her. She’s mine, too.”

He grinned. “Fine, a bargain for a bargaining creature. If she is truly yours, I cannot use her will. She will fight in your stead.”

I looked at Scharf and back at him.

“You can ask her,” he said, spinning his blade. “I will not break the terms. You know that.”

I pulled down one of her arms. “We have seconds. He wants you to fight.”

An unholy grin lit her face. “Great.”

“He’s a demi-god with a weapon that if it touches you, kills you. Don’t do this. This is my fight.”

She looked at me. “No, it isn’t.”

“I don’t want to watch you die! I did that already today.”

She summoned a travesty of a smile and suddenly looked to be as old as Whatever, a thousand years on her shoulders. “I signed up for that. A long time ago, Orange. All this time, our bargain was exactly backward. The general doesn’t die for her country. The soldier does.”

“That’s awful. You’re not a soldier. And I’m nobody’s general.”

“Just their princess?” This smile was gentler; more personal, still horrible. “Orange, you sent those goblins out for you.”

I keeled over, my stomach heaving. Had I known? I hadn’t wanted it to happen, planned for it to happen, but I asked for their help with a creature I knew was stronger than all of us. I sent them into this battle, and Dun had died.

I licked my lips, acrid bile wetting the back of my throat.

Scharf took the knife from my hand. “Let me do this.”

“To die for me.”

“No.” She pointed, encompassing the festival of scraggly nerds dressed in green. “For them.” She turned to Cool, the knife loosely held at her waist. “ Of course, ideally, I win.”

I grabbed her wrist. “Bargain, all the power of all the quests from all the goblins today for this fight.” If she was going in to fight for me, she was going to do it armed with whatever I could give her.

She flipped the knife and said, “Deal.”

A chime rang out. And then she staggered.

Oops.

How did a human interact with magic? And it was a lot of magic. These were questions I should have asked before this. Like always, I was looking before I was leaping blindly into a fight with a demi-god and maybe dooming her instead of helping.

“You okay?” I asked, ineffectually tugging her sleeve again.

She stretched her neck; I’d seen her sparring in her living room with the bag hung in the corner and knew she attended a local gym that consisted mostly of mats and angry sparring partners, but I didn’t know how good she was.

“Oh yeah. I’m great.” She was not generally overconfident, but I couldn’t help being a little worried.

I looked at the crumpled form of Dun and saw Mrs. Henderson standing over him, answering questions from the crowd. My heart leaped in my throat when Dun seemed to climb to his feet, but his movements were all wrong. Mrs. Henderson said, “He’s fine!”

I realized she was magically moving him. I ran over to her. “Find Hassan. He’ll have his car close. Put him in there.”

She met my eyes and dismissed me with a flick of her hand.

“Clear a space,” Cool said.

I spun. “Wait, I didn’t say deal. I’m changing the terms. She wins and you stop. We get the treasure back, and you stop leaching off humanity.”

For a second, the smug look left his face. “You know quite a lot for a beast.”

I grinned. “Thanks for confirming. Deal?”

It took a second for his rage to melt back into the arrogant mien he was wearing to fight. “Sure,” he said with a shrug.

He didn’t seem worried about losing.

Scharf rotated the knife in one hand, loosening her wrist. I hurried over to her. “Don’t let the spear touch you. Also, he goes into a battle frenzy. So, uh, watch out for that. Also he salmon leaps.”

Scharf blinked. “What have you been reading?”

“Irish epics. I don’t know what it means, but he defeated Ferdia with a salmon leap. And the spear. It’s always the spear.”

“I got this.” Scharf scrunched up her shoulders and let them fall and tested the blade without nicking herself. “I’m trying to tell myself that this is not justice, and he should go to jail. But I don’t hate your way, today.”

“Well good, ‘cause a jail would hold him for two seconds. Actually maybe not. They hate iron.”

She glanced at me. “Is that why there was a kettlebell in my bathtub?”

“Umm.”

Cool spun and she said, “Never mind.”

I took a step back and cleared the half of the volleyball court they were going to fight in with my earth power nudging everyone out of the way. There had been jousts and wrestling and contests all day, so I hoped this would just count as another one.

The two warriors squared off in the sand in an eerily similar stance from millennia apart. Cool stood with shield and spear in each hand, feet square. Scharf stood with her right foot behind her left and her hands in front of her face, left palm open and right holding the knife.

Cool made his first move, and in a blink, they locked together, knives hilt to hilt, staring into each other’s eyes over the crossed blades. The crowd oohed. Fortunately, magically enhanced, Scharf kept up with him. Just.

They disengaged and danced backward and the crowd cheered. When Cool charged again with a very similar move, Scharf didn’t bother with the knife but used her forearm to knock his knife arm aside and take hold of the front of his leather jerkin and kicked twice at his legs before kneeing him in the balls.

He gasped and uttered an oath as she dropped the knife to grab his spear hand and fold him in half, using his elbow as leverage. I blinked and felt a frisson of hope. In less than a second, she’d gotten him bent down ninety degrees at the waist, his arm straight behind him, his feet spread, trying to keep upright. I guess I shouldn’t have been surprised; we had thousands more years to perfect our combat.

Magic fast, she grabbed the spear on either side of his hand and twisted until it spun like a wheel. When he lost purchase, she put it over her knee and broke it in half. The crowd cheered again. As Cool roared more like a beast than a man, she froze for a second, staring at the two halves of the spears.

“Gimme!” I shouted, and she tossed them into the sand at my feet. Gingerly, I picked both halves up. Aside from the wicked metal blade, the hilt was suspiciously white, and I remembered reading about a bone spear. I had hoped it was a metaphor. Though if not, it proved this was Gáe Bolg, Cool’s one-hit-wonder spear. And she’d broken it in the first minute. I glanced at Scharf and said, “Whoa.”

They were circling again, Cool down to a dagger that was a much closer match for Scharf’s.

“I’ll take that, lassie,” a smooth voice said from behind me.

I leaped away from whoever was behind me, almost taking someone out with the tip. I hissed and pointed it into the sand at my feet.

Cool fell for the same move again. He attacked and Scharf deflected the blade and kicked at him again. She didn’t seem to make contact with his balls as he danced out of the way, but he didn’t get in a hit either.

I glanced at the owner of the voice, a young man with blond ringlets and otherwordly eyes.

“What is that boy getting up to?” he said in a lilting Irish accent.

As he asked that, he reached for the spear.

“Mine!”

“Ah, púca, you do not know what you hold.”

“I do, and my champion won it, so it’s mine now. Maybe you could call off your dog.”

His eyes flared and I winced, remembering too late not to antagonize grumpy fairies.

“You would be Lugh,” I said, brazening it out. “The point of all of this.”

“He said to come, so I have. Tis a grand festival.”

Cool and Scharf met again. This time, Scharf couldn’t get in close. He angled his short sword across his chest, and she met that with the knife. She spent the next few seconds kicking out at his legs until his concentration wavered, and she shoved him backward.

I spun back to Lugh. “Stand him down, and I’ll call her off.”

He cocked his head. “He’s playing with her. He’ll be fine.”

Alarm flared in my gut. “But will she be?”

He shrugged, and his eyes lit up like a child on Christmas morning as Cool made another move, though that was definitely the wrong metaphor for a Celtic fairy. When I turned, they were in the same dance, and Lugh clapped along to a girl with a ribbon on the other side of the volleyball court.

“Do you know what your son has been doing?”

“A fine craic,” he said, which was an untranslatable Gaelic word for something like fun or merriment.

“No, not a fine craic, a fine thief! He stole a bunch of art and stuck it away in fairyland. It doesn’t belong to him.”

“Wouldn’t they now belong to him if he took them?” Lugh asked, genuinely curious.

I whirled back to the sand as they crashed together again. Cool pushed hard, almost bowling through Scharf as their knives met. In a flurry of sand and cheers, she was on her back.

I closed one eye, knowing I could not stand to see her injured and unable to look away. But almost immediately, she windmilled her feet in the air, keeping him away from her until she got her hands in the sand, one on her knife, and propelled herself to her feet by heaving forwards, using the extra momentum to unbalance him. He had to stagger away.

I should have had a little faith. Most FBI agents I met were paper pushers, but Scharf had come out of the army and clearly kept up the training. Her personal obsession to be moving at all times also helped.

I turned back. “Aren’t you the god of justice? Where’s the justice in ‘finders keepers?’”

He scratched his golden curls. “But that is the law of the land.”

I rolled my eyes. “Save me from prehistoric morality. That is not the law of the land! Not this land, which he’s standing on. And so are you.”

Scharf grunted, a tiny sound in the melee, but I knew it. It was pain.

Cool came in with an outside slash, too quick for her to counter, and his razor-sharp blade opened her knife arm from her shoulder to her elbow. He finished the slice with a spin and a roar, planting his feet again, and Scharf staggered back. The crowd stopped cheering and started to murmur.

“You guys, she’s hurt,” somebody yelled.

Scharf stood, breath heaving, knife down, as Cool attacked again. Quicker than the eye, she whipped it up and broke the skin under his right ear as she dodged, and he staggered past her. The exhaustion was a feint. Blood sprayed into the sand and a girl screamed. Somebody stepped onto the sand, and I pushed him back, though it took all the magic I had. I had given the rest to Scharf. I saw Grimm cowering behind a stroller near the edge of the crowd.

I whirled on Lugh. “This isn’t a game. This isn’t your world. You’re in my city breaking my laws. Give it back.”

Lugh sighed. “And what will you give me in return?”

What could he want that I could give? I thought of Kristy and her art, of the pixies fighting for their lives for human amusement. I wasn’t going to stop people from sacrificing for their gods, but I could make sure they knew what was happening. “You can keep running your Ponzi scheme, sucking the life out of people if they consent to it. On both sides.” His eyes lit. I added. “Nobody gives an inch of power without, um, their informed consent.”

“What is that?” He looked slightly horrified.

“They know exactly what they are getting into and all the implications and always get a way to come back.”

“You spoil everything.”

“Deal?” The word never felt so heavy in my mouth.

“What if she loses?”

“She doesn’t lose. We both know Cool has,” I said with a bravado I did not believe in the slightest.

He examined his fingernails.

The combatants crashed together again, and Scharf hooked a leg around him to land him in the dirt.

An eerie silence fell over the pit. Cool stared up at Scharf and then at his own blood on his fingertips. Then he seemed to grow. When he was towering a foot over Scharf, I realized he didn’t just seem to grow—he literally grew. And grew. And grew. His pale skin turned a mottled red.

The rage.

Scharf mopped at the blood coursing down her arm and then switched the knife to her left hand. Cool went for her, forgetting his weapon, dropping it in his rush to get his hands around her throat. When he made contact, she went over backward and hit the ground with a thwack. I heard the distant rush of sirens and winced.

Somebody stopped being fooled that we were playacting, but I couldn’t take my eyes off Scharf choking on the ground. She got both hands between his arms and twisted, breaking his hold, and slammed him into the ground beside her.

I spun back to Lugh. “Say deal, damn you.”

Cool rolled into a backward somersault that brought him to his feet behind her and reached for her knife.

She jumped into a crouch and drove into him like a linebacker in the last scrimmage of the game, grabbing him around the legs and throwing him to the ground. On the way down, his knife carved into her left shoulder with a fresh spray of blood.

As she staggered back to mop at the blood, he leaped off the ground, legs akimbo and arms outstretched. His chest came toward the ground first so he was temporarily exactly horizontal, then sent a wave down his body as he dipped his head toward Scharf, ending with the flick of his feet, looking almost like a fish through the air.

“Hunh, that’s a salmon leap.”

As his knife swiped down, Scharf grabbed the shield he’d dropped and drove it into his throat, severing his head. Screams engulfed the volleyball courts, mine joining everyone else, including Scharf. She let out a high-pitched squeal for half a second before she gulped and got back to her feet and back in her stance, her hands shaking just a little bit. The crowd started running.

I felt a hand on my shoulder and screamed again.

“Bíodh sé ina mhargadh. Margadh!” Lugh shouted.

“Are you saying bargain?”

“Margadh!” he repeated.

I frowned. The Scots Gaelic word for a bargain was, well, bargain. One of the rare words to survive the Saxons, but apparently not in Ireland. “Does that mean deal?”

“Yes.”

When Cool’s torso moved, I screamed again. He felt for his head and plopped it on his neck. Scharf was tying her arm in a green scarf of some football team someone had given her, but she whirled and dove for the knife again.

Cool fixed his head back on his neck and shuddered as he shrunk back to small and pale and human again. “Put your knife away, warrior, I am defeated.” He looked at me and snapped his fingers.

I raised an eyebrow until something whacked me on the head, and I screamed again. I stooped down to see a spoon, the twin of the one Scharf had shown me.

I glanced at her, wondering if she had tossed it, but she had both hands full. Then something else whacked me on the shoulder, a plate. It miraculously—or rather magically—didn’t break on contact.

I ran for the nearest tree at the edge of the court as the rest of the treasure poured out of the air. Fortunately, very few people watched; they were too busy running from the murder they’d all witnessed. Someone was screaming about child appropriateness at a family festival and gruesome entertainment.

I looked at Cool. “Thanks.”

25

Lugh shook his head on the other side of the pile of arts and crafts. “Tis beautiful work. A pity it stays locked up.”

“You called me thief,” Cool said. “It isn’t thieving to liberate what was always ours.”

“Just because you’re strong enough to take it doesn’t mean it belongs to you anymore. We count a little different now.”

Lugh cocked his head. “Tisn’t ours because we took it. Tis ours because it’s made for us.” He pointed at a goblet that had a replica of his shield printed on it.

I remembered a case full of orange scones at a bakery in my hometown that I’d claimed because it had my name on it. Maybe this was something all magical creatures had in common. Of course, I was six at the time.

When Lugh reached for it, I shook my head. “Whoever owned it before, you bargained it away. It’s mine now.” I felt a weight on my shoulders and a distant chime. I twitched. “I have got to stop saying shit like that.”

Scharf rolled her head carefully, bones in her neck cracking sickenly, and sat down on one of the fine chairs. “Somebody has to pay. You did it. You pay.”

“She is right,” I said.

“No!” Cool said.

“Tis a strange justice,” Lugh said, his voice calm. “The weak locking up the strong and there they sit to rot, but as the púca said, we are in her world.”

“You can’t make me!” Cool said jumping back.

“Dun can,” I said.

“Who?”

“The little brownie you killed.”

“He can’t. He’s dead,” Cool said with perfect unconcern.

“You were a guest in his home, and you brought harm to him. You broke the laws of guesting.”

Cool opened his mouth, and his father said in an entirely different tone, “Ye broke the laws of guesting?”

This was apparently a greater crime than salmon kicking the life out of Scharf.

“Father, no.”

“Ye were bested. Ye take the consequences.”

Scharf pointed to the chair next to her and said, “Sit.”

Cool looked at me, at Scharf, at his father. He sat.

I thought about a fairy locked up in a cage of iron. I didn’t know how long sentences were for stealing an exhibit’s worth of art from the Smithsonian, but it probably measured in decades. It would be a blip in his long life, but it wouldn’t be a pleasant blip.

I dug the shield pin Kristy made and offered it to Lugh. “Here. As consolation.”

His eyes lit again. I wasn’t fooled by the childlike mien. “Tis a gorgeous working.”

“Tell that to Christie. She’s at the booth…”

I glanced over. The tables had cleared out, and police were approaching. “Well, she was over there. Remember the rest of the bargain. Informed consent. Look it up.”

He looked up. “Look up to what?”

“Read about it. On the same doohicky you use to get to your game,” I clarified, not having much hope he would ever understand the concept that was still iffy to half of modern humans.

As he glanced at the sky, he turned in a full circle. Then he looked over the abandoned festival. “This really was a beautiful day. Tis good to see the old arts coming back. They’ll be needed.”

I was about to join Scharf in another chair but froze. “They’ll be needed? What? When?”

He just smiled the mysterious smile of ancient beings that seemed universal to djinn, dragons, goddesses, and fairies. That they knew something we didn’t know; but they also knew it wouldn’t help us any to know it.

“Tis your city, púca, we concede. A fair bargain.”

Lugh melted away. The police made it to the volleyball pit where Scharf sat with her badge out saying, “Stand down. Situation under control. The FBI is on the way.”

The psychic drifted by wearing a sweeping gray skirt that seemed to move on its own.

I jumped up to keep her away from the police. “Did you foresee this?” I asked with an edge to my voice I could not banish.

She gave a short, sharp laugh.

“You aren’t a psychic. Are you? You aren’t even human.”

She chuckled and said in an accent that matched Lugh’s, “I get nervous when that old bastard gets an idea into his head.”

“So what, you were just going to stand back until when?”

“I am a defender. I didn’t know this city already had one.”

She drifted away. A defender.

“The Morrigan! You’re the Morrigan, the battle fairy, god person.” She was Cú Chulainn’s compatriot, another of the fae folk. If we’d had the Morrigan on our side, what on earth did they need me for?

She smiled the same ancient smile.

The city already has a defender. “Wait a minute, not me! I can’t defend a city. I am so far down the pecking order you would laugh if you even understood that reference.”

“You claimed DC. DC is the capital of this nation.”

“That’s not how this works.”

“That is exactly how this works.”

The Morrigan, whose hair was decidedly curlier than it had been a minute before and much blonder, turned to Scharf and said, “A blessing on you, woman warrior. A worthy daughter.”

Scharf froze. “Uh.”

I mouthed, Don’t say thanks!

“Um. Okay.”

I cringed. That was acceptance. Scharf had been blessed by the Morrigan. That could not be a good thing.

The lady in question was very pleased. She laughed as more screaming sirens announced the arrival of more law enforcement. She disappeared, and Scharf grunted. She pulled the scarf away from her arm, and it was whole without a single scratch.

“Who was that?” she asked as she hastily ripped off her bloody Quantico sweatshirt, revealing a dark blue FBI t-shirt. Her wardrobe definitely had a theme.

Giudice got out of the back of one car with Carlisle. The men I vaguely recognized from the art crimes unit stalked after him.

Carlisle said, “Cool? You found the treasure, uh, evidence?”

Scharf crossed her arms. “Place this man under arrest. He was the thief.”

“What?” Carlisle said. He pivoted to Cool, the man he’d brought into the task force. “What?”

I poked Cool’s side. “Say it.”

“Oh all right fine, I confess,” Cool said with an insouciant smile.

All the blood drained from Carlisle’s face as the big boss smacked his hands together.

I went invisible and stuck my tongue out at them.

EPILOGUE

We stood at the edge of Fort Ward. Scharf held Dun’s body wrapped in a couple of shopping bags from the back of Hassan’s car. Hassan and Amina stood at the Visitor’s Center out of earshot looking at Civil War-era tchotchkes.

With a wave, I opened the armory, and Scharf put him down in the dirt. Mrs. Henderson grabbed the bags to reveal Dun. His skin was gray, not brown. His sightless eyes seemed to stare at me, accusing. I gulped and looked away.

Mrs. Henderson started walking away.

I turned. “Shouldn’t we… bury him?”

She laughed. “There won’t be anything left of him in another day. We don’t decay, we fade.”

“Fade to what?”

A tiny smile lit her features; it was not a happy expression. “If I knew that…” She stared at Dun. “No one knows that.”

“Shouldn’t we… say something?” I persisted.

She looked down. “What?”

“I don’t know, ‘Rest in peace?’”

Mrs. Henderson thought about that and just said, “Rest.”

“Rest, Dun,” I repeated, choking back tears. It didn’t feel like nearly enough.

Scharf looked around and said, “On behalf of a grateful nation, thank you for your honorable and faithful service.”

It sounded like something she’d said before; like something had often been said before. I nodded. That sounded right.

He had served this nation and tended to one of our memories. And now he was a memory. I wiped away a tear. I didn’t care who had cast the final blow. He had died because of me, and Scharf had almost died because of me.

As we started back toward the car, I fought the guilt, fear, and grief that had swamped me since we’d left the Mall. I’d said goodbye to Astrid who had collected a prize for best bakery item, and art crimes had begun processing the arts and crafts. They would be back on display within the month.

Cool had gone to booking after confessing to the crime. I put Cool’s spear down beside Dun, feeling that an old fort in a war where a lot of Irish had died fighting in a land far away would be a fitting resting place for it. Already Dun looked more transparent.

I didn’t have a mirror to know what my face looked like, but I guessed it would be something like the ancient ones. I had thought danger to my own life was horrible; sending others in my stead was an order of magnitude worse.

“Our next NTEPUEL meeting is tomorrow,” Scharf said as we hiked back to Amina and Hassan.

“I don’t know if I can do this again,” I said to her, feeling the weight of what my words had wrought.

She rested a hand on my shoulder, achingly gently.

“I’m not kidding,” I said. “I’m going to get someone else killed. I can’t do this again.”

She dropped her hand. “Who better? How would you feel knowing that meeting was happening and not being there?”

I wanted to say yes. I would be fine if I left this whole project to others. But was it true? Could I bake treats and grant boons for goblins and leave this to… to who?

The Morrigan had said I’d claimed the entire country. It was bad enough when I’d claimed all the goblins. But everybody? There was no way! I gritted my teeth. I’d told Astrid I didn’t want to do this for the rest of my life.

“I don’t know,” I finally settled on.

Scharf nodded sagely. “You know what? Skip the meeting. It’s fine.”

“No.”

She just nodded, and I realized she was as hesitant as I was. Before, she’d had the bargain, and it had simplified things for both of us. I owed her. She collected. But now I owed her nothing. She had no hold on me. The government thought they did, but I had been pardoned of any crime.

“I don’t know,” I repeated, and she nodded again.

“There are things you won’t ever know. It’s just a gray mess all the way down. But ultimately, we recovered the treasure. The art! And we punished the guilty.”

“And all it took was a brownie dying!”

Her hand landed heavily on my shoulder. “He chose to fight.”

“That doesn’t change the fact that he shouldn’t have been there at all. That I brought him there and put him in harm’s way.”

“What would you do, sit at home and bake muffins?”

I smirked. “What did I tell you about making jokes?”

“What joke?” she asked earnestly.

I looked out at the cannons and the fort and the ancient spear in the dirt, evidence of centuries of conflict that wound tighter and tighter around me with each case. “I hate muffins.”

The End

ABOUT THE AUTHOR

Kyler Anne Ford writes brainy, funny, feminist, and diverse speculative fiction that explores what it means to be human, the old myths we tell ourselves to help us sleep at night, and our dreams of what’s next. Learn more at kyleranneford.com.
